JOTTINGS BY AN OLD

JOURNALIST.

" Delightful."

This as sub-head attracted my attention to the Burtonwood "Parish Magazine" rhapsody in last week's "Examiner." Of course, I very heartily and sincerely echo the congratulations pouring in upon the good Vicar, whose eye is not dimmed nor his natural force abated, but when he leads to the inference that the secret of perpetual youth lies in abstention from flesh meat and tobacco (not to mention alcoholic liquor, from an advertisement of which he- con- descends to borrow a phrase, perhaps on the Salvation Army principle that the devil should not be allowed to monopolise the best tunes), the old query as to whether life is worth living in- evitably recurs. Points of View.

Recurs, I mean, to the mind of the ordinary mortal, leaving out-of account the incorrigible one who openly and unashamed wrillows amid the fleshpots, pipe and Oath; at hand, with "a short life and a merry one" as his motto. Said one in the hearing of a confirmed old bachelor, "I wonder how it is the married live longer than the single?" "They don't," growled the other, "it only seems longer." It strikes me that this is capable of application to the matter in hand. "Without alcohol!" No deprivation so far as lam personally concerned. "Without flesh meat !" Hardly. .any alternative for the time being—almost beyond the reach of many of us at present prices, alas! alas! a thousand times alas! and one more deep-drawn alas on to that! "Without tobacco !" What thousands of weaker men than the Rev. A. M. Mitchell will more or less impatiently and without hesi- tation draw the .line here—moreover (happy thought in these times of abnormal prices) does not "the weed" allay the pangs of hunger? Faddists.

If I may be quite candid, I find some of these well-meaning vegetarians uncomfortable folk to be on terms of intimacy with. The other day I made the acquaintance of an apparently inter- esting young fellow, and had come thoroughly, to enjoy his company, when, to my dis- appointment and disgust, out popped the cloven hoof. He proved to be one of the most rabid of vegetarians and anti-everythings. I has- tily retreated on the plea of an engagement else- where; but he followed me up with the usual persistence, plying the ancient arguments with which I have long been familiar but which fail to convince, and finally he thrust into my re- luctant hands a formidable bundle of that same appalling literature. 'Just one more instance of misguided zeal. On Saturday morning Mrs. A.O.J. asked me to do an errand in the town— never mind what; suffice it that it was not wholly unconnected with the meat which per- isheth, and that it was intended for that day's dinner. It was my ill-luck to encounter a cer- tain Nonconformist minister of my acquaint- ance who is an extreme pacifist, holding the doctrine of non-resistance and prepared to accept the logical consequences. There and then he mounted his hobby and threw down the gage, and at it we went in the street hammer and tongs for two precious hours, administering to each other several varieties of beans. Of course, when I reached home there was a little discussion of a different character—but that is another story.

" Cowlady." This word, quoted last week from a West Sussex advertisement, is a much older one than might be supposed. I well remember as a youngster hearing it used by an old dame while chiding her erring spouse. "Well, this I will say," interposed the penitent one in conciliatory toxes, "you're a lady." "Humph 1" exclaimed the other, refusing to be flattered, "a cowlady." "Anyhow," pursued the old fellow, "you've a gentleman for your husband." So I heard " cowlady " between 50 and 60 years ago. Wanted. • _ _ .

Said an embarrassed speaker, whispering to a supporter on the platform, "Quick, I want a -word." !tight-o !" was the cheerful response, "what word do you want?" So "Farm and Home" deems "cowlady" as ridiculous as "cow- woman" is ugly. In like izaanner " chair- lady " will never do; yet "chairwoman" we cannot away with. I always overcame that particular difficulty by simply stating that Mrs. or Miss So-and-So presided; and what's the matter with "lady president"? Now we are all on the look-out for suitable synonyms for "mayor" for application to chairmen of county and urban and rural councils, and fearful and wonderful indeed are some of the suggestions thus far forthcoming.

Horrible Burial Custom. The other week I wrote once more in favour of cremation as incomparably the best mode of disposing of the dead. Since then I have been thinking of the towers of silence on Malabar Hill, Bombay, as affording the greatest possible contrast. The Parsees refuse to dispose of human remains either by burial or burning'; they have an objection fo defiling the three elements, in fact, so a dead body is taken to the towers of silence, carried to a raised platform, where the covering is removed, and lift to the vultures which swarm, in the surrounding trees. The bones are afterwards cast into a common pit, where chemical treatment reduces them to ashes. The Parsee is disgusted at the idea of earth burial, with its slow corruption.

The Sin-eater. In our own country so comparatively recently as the seventeenth century, a not uncommon figure at a funeral was that of a "sin-eater.' John Aubrey, the antiquary, records (the M.S. is in the British Museum) that "In the county of Hereford was an old custome at Funeralls to hire poor People who were to take upon them the sinnes of the Party deceased. One of them, he was a long, leane, ugly, lamentable poor raskal, I remember lived, in a cottage on the Rosse highway." This accommodating old fellow was handed bread, beer and sixpence, and was required to consume the food, "- in con- siderafion whereof he took upon him, ipso facto, all the sinnes of the defunct." . I imagine that in quite a number of cases it would be cheap at the price—though doubtless there are even now some who would be ready to take such a job on.

Curiosity at Funerals. At NelSon, where I lived a little while, the then curate of St. Mary's wrote thus in the " Parish Magazine" of what he deemed "idle curiosity at funerals ":—" It is impossible to conceive of a time that ought to be more sacred and more solemn. Different people have different ideas as to what is seemly and un- seemly, but I cannot look upon the crowds that assemble about the house when a funeral is leaving as other than a gross intrusion upon private sorrows." I never have believed that it is nearly so bad as that, and I fancied at the time that those of whom this was written would be grieved at having their motives so misunder- stood. However, as the reverend gentleman observed, there is room for difference of opinion here.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OVER-PRODUCTION OF POISON.

Some people produce uric-acid twice as fast as others, and an overload of this poison is a -serious thing for anyone. It comes in different ways, but the most productive causes are over- exertion and eating too much, particularly of meat.

Some allowance should be made to those suffering from uric-acid complaints, for they can't help being nervous, morose, cross, sus- piciouq, headachy, dizzy, at times, and racked with all sorts of queer pains. The rheumatic sufferer knows what real pain is like,, and it is no wonder he grows old too fast—and sooner or later develops heart trouble, sciatica, gravel, dropsy, or hardening of the arteries.

The sensible thing to do is to take warning at the first sign that uric-acid is developing—eat less meat, and not too Much of any food. Drink plenty of both milk and water. Take regular exercise, and get eight hours' sleep seven nights a week. Use Dean's Backache Kidney Pills to repair the weakened kidneys and help them filter uric- acid from the blood. You can't be really well while an excess of this poison remains in the system.

DEATH AFTER GLORY.

Military Medallist Dies Of

Wounds.

PTE. A. HUGHES' ILL-LUCK

Following the announcement in "The Ex- aminer" last week that Private Albert Hughes, of the Buffs Regiment, had been awarded the Military Medal, his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Hughes, of 86, Longford-street, were be- sieged with congratulations, which they "must be sure to pass on to your gallant son." This week it is oar painful duty to record that the feeling of triumph and joy has been short- lived, for on Monday an intimation vas re- ceived that Hughes has since died of wounds. Bravely he lived, bravely he died, as the fol- lowing note from a hospital sister testifies: " Your son died on the 4t4 from abdominal wounds. He was brought to our hospital about one p.m., but was so severely wounded that there was no hope from the beginning, and he only lived about three hours. But he was so brave and patient right up to the lat." When Hughes wrote home mentioning the honour he had gained he was still in the firing line, and apparently he, received his .mortal wounds a few hours afterwards. His letter to his parents read as follows: "Just a short note to say I have been awarded the Military Medal. I was recommended on July 3rd by our Colonel, and the good news was told me on Monday, July 31st. Will you please inform my

===== 0002601_19160819_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
friends, especially at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church? I think I am the first one from Bow. soy-road to receive a distinctiOn. Our- ' firm ' now have one V.C., one D.C.M., and one Mili- tary Medal. I am writing this letter in the front line, without my tunic on, for it. is awfully hot." Private Hughes, who was formerly employed at the Aluminium Works, Bank Quay, would have been in the Army two years next month, and had seen more than twelve months of active service. He was a well-known member of the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, and was a teacher at the Sunday school connected with that church. As a boy he attended St. Ann's School. A brother, Willie Hughes, has been to France, but has returned to England for medical attention. Another brother, Hughie, is with the Royal Engineers. Their father is a blacksmith at Dallam Forge. -

LCE.-CPL. F. LITTL.EIN'OOD.

Shop Assistant Missing Since thdy 9th.

Lance-Corporal Frederick Littlewood, of UK. Lancashire Fusiliers, one of the three soldiee sons of Mr. and Mrs. W. H. Littlewood, 17, Laira-street, has been officially posted as missing after an engagement on July 9th. Lance-Corporal Littlewood, who is 21 years of age, joined the Army in October, of last Year, and has.been at the Front since May. lie was formerly employed as a shoo assistant at the Market Gate branch of the Maypole Dairy Co., Ltd., and was educated at Hamilton-street School. He formerly attended The Kent-street Mission, Latchford. The two soldier brothers are Private Robert Littlewood, of the South Lancashire Regiment, and Private Harold Littlewood, of the Monmouthshire Regiment, who was called up in March.

PRIVATE J. H. LESTER.

Reported Missing After Engage- ment On July Ist.

Mr. and Mrs. A. Lester, 19, Lord Nelson- street, have been informed by the War Office that their son, Private James Heniy Lester, of the Lancashire Fusiliers' was reported missing after an engagement on July Ist. News of his whereabouts is naturally anxiously awaited by his parents. Private Lester, who has been in the Army thirteen months and has two other brothers serving, is 19 years of age, and, previous to enlisting, worked for the Firth Co., Ltd. TOBACCO

For Warrington Men On Service En Egypt And France.

"NICE THAT SOMEONE THINKS

OF US ! "

Nearly 100 Warrington men are with the Rifle Brigade on active service in Egypt, and in response 'to an appeal for tobacco from some of them a consignment was despatched. All the men were drafted cut in November last from the South. Lancashire Battalions. The thanks of Captain Willeocks and the men ,in his charge are contained in the 'following acknowledgments : " Dear Mr. Lyon 'Whittle,—The consignment of `, cis.' has arrived safely and in splendid condition, and I have distributed them to the Warrington men, who have expressed their thanks to the subscriber's for the gift. This is the first gift these men have had since being drafted to us on 2nd November, 1915. The men are all 'from some battalion or other of the South Lancashires. Thank you for your letter to me. I quite realise your difficulty and appreciate your making an ,exception in the case of my men. We are having a strenuous time, and I believe this summer is alnlost a record for beat; it is' seldom below 100 degrees shade and usually quite 105 degrees. We have had it 112 degrees.—Thanking you very much, I am, yours very truly, E. F. WILLcocKs, Capt. 19th R. 8."

MANY THANKS FOR PARCEL OF SMOKES RECEIVED. "Needless to say all the B'hoys were more than delighted with the handsome gift from their own townsmen."—C. T. Cox, R.Q.M.S. "It is very nice to know that someone thinks of our wants and welfare whilst we are doing

our little bit out here. Again thanking you all."—R. C. Kinsey. "It gives me great pleasure in acknowledg- ing most welcome gift of cigarettes, which I appreciate very much. Hoping this finds all the dear old friends in Warrington in the best of health."—R. Norman, Cpl. "Hope the war is over before all the_smokes are finished. Good luck to all in Warrington!" —R. W. Nixon, Sergt. "They are a welcome gift and will help to remind us of home and the kind friends who have thought of us."—T. Green, Private; T. A. Jones, Corporal. "A good sold Woodbine is always welcome to the 'Tommy.' Hoping to be amongst you all very soon."—F. Young, Corporal. "Kindly accept very best thanks of ,two Warrington lads for the most welcome parcel of smokes kindly sent by friends at home. We are in the pink, and, like Johnnie Walker, 'still going strong.' We shall soon be back again, so cheero!"—G. Carman, Sergeant; J. T. Hassall, Rifleman. "We, the undermentioned men of the 'Battalion, Rifle Brigade, wish to thank you and all kind friends for the present of cigar- ettes that was sent to us. With best wishes to you and hoping you will convey our thanks to all, hoping it won't be long before we see the good old town again. We remain, your humble servants."-114 Private M. Flynn; 141 Private W. Renshaw; 134 Private T. Halli- gan. "I beg to thank you on behalf of myself and the remainder of the boys from Warrington for the welcome gift. It gives all the boys satisfaction to know that the many friends in Warrington have not forgotten them. God bless them all."-493 D. Callaghan, Corporal. AGREEABLY SURPRISED.

Colonial's Impressions Of Our Busy Borough.

ENTERPRISING BUSINESS MEN. "Hello, there! This is Watson, of the Chamber of Commerce!" "Oh, yes. We know what you want. We've -heard all about you. You're just the man we •Should like to see. Come rou,nd and you'll be Welcome." This is a typically terse and enthusiastic telephonic communication between Mr. A. -Sidney Watson (an Australian who has been •Spending a month with the Warrington Cham- ber of Commerce) and the business men in the town and district. Every factory head in the borough now knows Mr. Watson, and nearly every one has made use of him. Mr. Watson came to Warrington at the in- vitation of the Chamber of Commerce with a view to persuading manufacturers to take .Part in a scheme, the main phase of which is the compilation of an official register of British manufacturers in connection with the •caniPaign for the extension of British trade after_ the war. To effect this it took Mr. Watson just a month, and half an hour before he caught a train for Liverpool, where he is erabarking oh a similar enterprise, an " Examiner" interviewer had an interesting chat 'kith him on the eve of his departure. Mr. Watson was not glad to kick the dust of Warrington from his feet. "I am very 6°rrY to go," he said, "for I have had an agreeable if strenuous time in the town, and have been particularly successful with my work. The highest praise is due to the manu- facturers for realising the importance of the movement. It speaks well for their enterprise in, at least, letting the buyers throughout the world know what they are making. The War- rington Chamber, we know, welcomed and appreciated the value of the proposed publi- • cation and aceepted at once the proposal made through the secretaries of the Associated 'Chambers of Commerce. 'Of course, the general ,complaint about British manufacturers has neen that they are too conservative. I do not find this to be the case in Warrington, for Your manufacturers are very much alive to the possibilities of wresting trade from the enemy countries and also taking advantage of the openings in hitherto undeveloped markets, Snell ps Russia, France and Italy. Here there lS ample room for the enterprise of the engineering and wire-making firms. Every- where have been received most courteously, and in most cases, enthusiastically. "Another reproach of the British manufacturers is that they use only our own language as literature for foreign countries. In Warrington I have found that a large percentage of the manu- facturers long ago got into the way of employ- ing the particular foreign language needed to Persuade people to buy, and in that respect they are beyond reproach. At nearly all the '34 firms they have engaged the services of raen who thoroughly understand the language and the foreign markets, and, through this ability, have been able to develop their foreign trade extensively. - "THRIVING AND PROSPEROUS." '• "I have been extremely surprised at the great variety of industries existing in War- rington—you seem to make everything except White elephants—and to a visitor from the Dominions, to whom Warrington is terra incognita, it comes as a pleasant shock to see • such a wonderfully thriving and prosperous place. I found nearly every industry repre- sented, and, in particular, was agreeably sur- prised to learn that the shipbuilding industry had its incipiency here, and was glad to see, from the ships on the stocks at Sankey Bridges, that the industry had not died out." With our business concerns Mr. Watson is so Pleased that he remarked that in the future he Will always reckon himself to be the commercial -representative for Warrington, and will do all in his power to advance our interests through- out the world. With that observation he passed on to his im- Pressions of the town generally. Having con- eidered that the Corporation is thoroughly up- to-date, and spoken highly of the "magnificent 'buildings of public utility"—such as the Town Ilan, Library, Museum, and Baths—and his Satisfaction that there are several open breath- ing spaces, Mr: Watson said that in conversa- tion with aldermen and councillors, he had been iagreeably surprised to hear that we are well to the fore in town-planning schemes, and, no doubt, in the future, he added. Warrington will resemble a colonial city, with the additional in- terest of being tinged with antiquity. "War- rington compared very favourably with other Manufacturing towns—and I have been in Many," he declared. "In fact, I don't know of any other of its size which has such a general air Prosperity, well-being, and up-to-dateness. "A visitor from overseas, particularly one not used to things of antiquity, cannot help but be ira pressed by the efforts of the Warrington Nociety in' preserving some of the ancient land- * raarks—l refer particularly to the many tablets commemorating the birthplaces and residences of notable citizens of the past. I envy you the Old Academy. During the time I have been here I have spent several restful hours there, and the literary atmosphere one gets is very conducive as a respite from business worries.' From the ecclesiastical standpoint Warring- ton is also highly favoured. The many churches of all denominations have been well worthy of 5. ViSit. At the services I have been particularly IniPressed by the beautiful musio and the rorcible and able speaking of the ministers. At the Parish Church, for instance. I should say ..that the singing -is equal to anything I have neard in some of the large and more famous churches in other parts of the kingdom." , Mr. Watson gives us a clean slate in regard 'to our behaviour. "Notwithstanding the crowd- ing of the streets," lie said, "conduct is very good. The people seem to be exceptionally orderly, looking well-dressed, well-nourished, 41nd prosperous generally. I have been here for Ir,ochs, and, though I have had my eyes open all 2e time. I have never seen a drunken man in ,he street or anyone arrested. That speaks well •aoth for the people and the police administra- cion.,,

"THE PROBLEM OF THE COMMONWEALTH."

Women And The Vote.

SUGGESTIONS BY SIR A. H. CROSFIELD.

The following letter appeared in The Times" for Wednesday : Sir,—There is a very natural and in many cases justifiable tendency to relegate all poli- tical questions to those happy times when this war shall have been brought to a satisfactory conclusion. But there are some political questions that cannot be so dismissed; which on the contrary are of great and increasing urgency owing to the very fact of the war. Of these supreme in importance is the great ques- tion of the reconstruction of Imperial organi- sation and government with which the Empire, will be confronted immediately the war is over. That this question is exercising the minds of statesmen in the Mother Country and in the Colonies is already abundantly clear. We have had the Prime Minister's memorable reference to that fact in a speech to his con- stituents, and there have been other very sig- nificant references to it by statesmen at home and abroad. Moreover, a little reflection may enable us by process of elimination to form a fairly accurate idea of the direction which their deliberations are probably taking.

An Imperial Parliament.

But the great mass of our people, absorbed as they are in the vast issues of the war, have hitherto received no light and leading on this all-important problem.' For that very reason I am certain that anyone who reads the admir- able work by-Mr. Curtis entitled "The Problem of the Commonwealth" will agree that Mr. Curtis has rendered a public service of the highest value in urging, as he has done, that the Mother Country, conjointly with the Colonies, ought here and now to be giving full consideration to every aspect of this great pro- blem. Mr. Curtis argues with force, lucidity, and cogency that the only satisfactory solution of the problem will be found in the institution of an Imperial Parliament with an Imperial Cabinet responsible to it—such an Imperial Parliament and Cabinet being entirely distinct from our present Parliament and Cabinet.

The Imperial Parlianni-;;;;;Id,-;:i;;;Points out, be chiefly concerned with such questions of paramount importance as (1) Imperial diplo- macy, (2) Imperial defence, (3) immigration. Other questions of comparatively minor impor- tance which might come within its sphere of influence and control need not be alluded to here—with one exception, which would neces- sarily be included under the first and second headings. If the greatest world asset that can be created, after the war, as some set-off to its appalling sacrifices and deficit, is the guarantee of international peace among civilised nations by means of international co-operation backed by international force, then beyond question the Imperial Parliament would represent the whole Empire in this priceless achievement. • A Chance To Solve Suffrage Question.

Under such a system our present Parliament would confine itself, as do the Parliaments of the Dominions, to domestic legislation, and would, of course, have exclusive control of all questions in that sphere. May not such a solution of the Imperial problem possibly afford a solution of a domestic problem which has given rise to one of the thorniest controversies of modern times—woman suffrage? Women have throughout the present great crisis rendered magnificent service to the national cause, and it is contended that with the wide consequent extension of their spheres of activity which has taken place there has been a corresponding development of their opinion on the question of the suffrage. If that be so—if the women of England are now dis- posed to assume this privilege, and at the same time added responsibility—will there not in that case be an opportunity to settle the controversy over the suffrage by giving women the vote on the same terms as men for the Domestic Parliament while basing the Imperial Parliament on an exclusively male electorate? Such a solution would at any rate get rid of the objections to woman suffrage urged by those who feel that questions of diplomacy, and peace and war, more naturally come with- in the province of, as they would therefore be dealt with by, men.—Yours, &c., A. H. CROSPIELD. 41, West Hill, Highgate, N., August 12th.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN AND THE REGISTRATION

BILL.

Their Unanswerable Claim To Enfranchisement.

Mr. Asquith's statement on the Registration Bill has given rise to widespread support in the Press of the case for Women's Suffrage. The "Observer," the." Daily Express," the "Daily News" and the "Manchester Guardian" have all expressed the view that the claim for the enfranchisement of women is, in the words of the Prime Minister, "not only reasonable, but unanswerable." Mr. Asquith does not claim that the present Government proposal for deal- ing with the register provides a settlement of

questions of the franchise, or even of registra- tion; it is merely an emergency measure, de- signed to supply, if and when the occasion arises, a register upon which a general election can take place. No new class of voters is added, and all final settlements of franchise problems —which must include, besides votes for soldiers and sailors, a measure of enfranchisement for women—are to be left for a later and less abnor- mal time.

The following statement defines the attitude of the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies with regard to the present Bill : "In view of the national position as set forth by the Prime Minister in his speech of August 14th, 1916, the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies will abstain from pressing for an amendment to include women in the new Registration Bill, provided that the question of altering the basis of the franchise be not raised by other amendments or alterations to tile Bill. In deciding on this course, the National Union is postponing to its sense of the urgent national need of unity, its undiminished resolve to obtain for women the full rights of citizenship, which are more than ever necessary, having regard to the problems of reconstruction bound to arise after the war."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 19, 1916.

de soie will also lead as summer declines. Striped and flowered taffetas have had a great success this summer, more especially in delicate contrasts for evening wear, such as pink and white, and pink and delicate green. The Influence of Colours.

An authority on colours recently stated that every individual has his or her own colour sense, which it was harmful to affront, and he deprecated the prevalence of wearing black, which, he said, was in many instances detri- mental to health. By wearing it 'continually every organ and function of the body may sus- tain injury. It decreases the working power of the lungs, and is to numbers of people an evil tint; to be avoided if possible. White, on the contrary, is healthy and hygienic, and aids the body in its efforts to throw off waste matter and , to resist disease. • Children's Food.

Speaking at the Mansion House, Sir Thomas Barlow said that mothers were often not alive to the need for making food attractive and palatable for growing children. They did not want a lot of red meat, but what they did have should be well cooked and plenty of vegetables and fresh fruit. Bananas and oranges had a very good effect on the digestion. Jam, valuable as it was, was not so good as fresh fruit for children of the humbler classes, who were abso- lutely certain to suffer if the importation of fruit was 'interfered with. Filth and Flies. •

There is an old Spanish proverb which says: " Into a closed mouth flies never enter." But the fact is that the flies don't have to enter— they do their work in a more dangerous fashion. They are born in. filth, bred in filth; and carry filth with them wherever they go, whether it be to the baby's bottle, to the market, to the dairy, or to. your table. They leave a trail of filth, disease, suffering and death behind them. Poached Eggs.

A deep saucepan should be used, and the water should reach the boiling point before the egg is carefully dropped in. Some cooks squeeze not more than two or three drops of lemon-juice into the water, and always use a tablespoonful of salt. A full minute should poach the egg sufficiently, when it is lifted out with the skimmer and laid upon the square of toast already prepared on a hot plat- ter. Some of the best cooks claim that when several eggs are poached at once they are not so good as when each is done separately. Eggs may be poached in an almost perfect sphere by givting to the water a rapid rotary motion with a spoon or fork, and dropping the eggs in the heart of the whirlpool thus formed.

"It is really a misfortune in civilised life that home flies do not sting or bite. If they did, we should all be as uneasy in their presence as we ought to be."—Margaret McKillep; in the August "Mothers' Magazine."

THE HUMAN ELEMENT.

Eliminating Discomfort And

===== 0002601_19160819_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
Discontent In Factories.

WELFARE SUPERVISORS AND THEIR DUTIES.

Mr. B. Seebohm Rowntree here contributes his

Second Article on a Vital Subject.

In my last article I discussed very briefly the question of improving factory conditions in general. I want to say something about the work of welfare supervisors. Employers hardly recognise the importance of a girl's first introduction to our factories. It may make all the. difference, if instead of being hurriedly engaged by a foreman, her first inter- view is with the welfare supervisor. The latter, after ascertaining her qualifications, and decid- ing to recommend her for employment, can have a talk with her about her future, and try to interest her in it, making her feel both that the firm intends to do "the square thing" by her, and that she must do "the square thing" by the firm. After this, for a time at least, the welfare supervisor should keep closely in touch with the newcomer, visiting her at the end of a day or two to see lor she is getting on, and inquiring whether she has any difficulty that can be removed, and after this paying her ' periodical visits so long as may be necessary. If the welfare supervisor has the right person- ality, this will make the girl feel from the start that she has a friend in the factory, who cares for her individually, who not only wants her to "make good," but will help her to the utmost of her power. Great care must 4 of course, be taken in the selection of the supervisor. She should_ have a real love for girls, and be methodical aiid of businesslike habits, and she should in the best sense of the word be a lady. For it will be her business to keep in touch with all the girls under her charge. They should feel that she is their friend.

There are a number of causes which may ad- versely affect the happiness and the efficiency of women workers, which a welfare supervisor can remove. For instance, a girl may be worried by ill-health a home. This worry will prevent her from attaining her normal output. But a chat with the welfare supervisor will often lead to some means being found for relieving the strain upon the girl's mind. This may take the form of a suggestion from the supervisor that the firm might make a small advance to meet the abnormal expenses of the household, if the case proves to be genuine. On the other hand, the mere fact that someone at the works has sympathised with the girl about her home trouble will make her feel herself about to be a member of the factory family. Better Type of Worker.

It would be difficult to enumerate all the direct- and indirect influences which such a worker exercises over a factory. But one thing is certain. Her work not only increases the happiness, health and efficiency of the girls under her charge, but it tends to attract a better type of worker. Many employers can show how they have been able to obtain girls with better education, and from better• homes, through the work of welfare supervisors at the factory. Rough, unskilled labour is seldom economical in the long run. And everything that tends to increase the personal responsi- bility and the efficiency of employes will help to give him a higher rate of output. A point that arises in appointing a welfare supervisor is, of course, the cost of such an officer. But this expenditure should be considered not merely as an additional outlay, but as a legitim- ate expense for improving the comfort and effi- ciency of the staff. A' small firm can generally secure a suitably trained person for £2 per week. Larger firms should pay from £3 to .25 per week, if they desire a worker conversant with all •the subtle difficulties that arise in handling large numbers of women.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
Not Much Cost.

If there are 500 girls working in a factory and the management pay £l5O per year for such a supervisor, she is costing the firm less than lid. per worker per week. If the average rate of wages is 12s. per week the supervisor is costing the firm 1 per cent. of the annual wages bill. If they are paying their girls an average of £1 per week she will only cost 0.6 per cent. of the wages bill. If, as a result of a more contented personnel, each girl does not produce far more that lid. of extra output per week then something is very much amiss. It is certain that such an officer is an economy in every sense of the word. She saves the manager from worrying over the thousand and one points that can be dealt with by women far better than by the best business man. Consequently, she frees the executives for more important work. It is clear that the whole success of welfare supervision will depend upon two things— firstly, the employer's recognition of its impor- tance, and, secondly, the personality of welfare supervisor. It is ..not easy to find the right woman for this work. The Welfare Department of the Ministry of Munitions has prepared a panel of ladies qualified to undertake it.

After more than twenty years' experience of welfare supervision in my own factory, I am thoroughly convinced of the wisdom of appoint- ing welfare supervisors where large numbers of girls are employed.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. J. R. CLYNES, M.P.

Says "A Word" To Workmen And To Employers.

Mr. J. R. Clynes, Lancashire district secre- tary of the National Union of General Workers, sends us a proof of his report for the quarter ended June 24th. There are points, he inti- mates, upon which he wishes to say a word both to members and employers. Men—Look at the Trenches and Hospitals.

"Workmen on munitions," he continues, "who are earning comparatively good. wages should think more and more of the men who have gone from their work for the hardships of the trenches and the burdens and risks of a soldier's life. Not all munition workers are well paid, or fairly treated, and their claims must be pressed. But the man with the worst industrial employment is better off than a poldier can be with the best conditions on active ,gervice in modern war. The man in the trenches has little pay and no overtime rates. his hours are regulated only by his capacity to endure, and his food and shelter are often worse than a badly-paid 'labourer can buy. Strenuous work should, therefore, be the first duty of workmen as their contribution to hastening the end of the war. And no strikes should be tolerated when men's claims can get a fair hearing. Any workman who is tempted to do less than his best should call at a military hospital, where the sight of his mates will re- mind him that whoever does wrong in this war workmen should do right." Employers--Look to Your Duty.

"The appeals made by low-paid workmen for redress against the burden of the-high cost of living deserve better treatment from the Government and from employers. The 2s. or 3s. advance given in the first year of the war has been more than wiped out by the oppressive increase, in the cost of commodities. Arbitration Courts have reached a point where they now concede little, and they appear to have fixed a maximum of advance which leaves wages far below the minimum required to pur- chase the necessaries of life. This will not do.. "The Government can exact loyalty and the best service by reasonable treatment of real grievances. If something better is not done it is natural that workmen should act upon the view that if capitalists, shipowners, and employers are entitle-el to use trading oppor- tunities arising from the war to make profit, workmen must be free to use the opportunities arising from the great demand for their labour, and exact the, highest rates which the labour market can yield. Workmen should never for- get the overpowering military necessities, and toil to make the service of the soldier success- ful, but the Government and employers have also their duty,which if readily done would help effectively to allay the troubles which trade unions have all along tried to prevent." The Union has 130.000 members. About 40,1111 i). ililuiing ,Ono n-nr•^A-Ire tad, are serving in the Army or Navy.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Leisured Women to the Rescue.

An appeal has lately been made to 'women of the leisured classes to come forward and help in the making of shells and other munitions of war. Women operatives have given up their pre-war work in the factories to such an extent in order to help in the arsenals, that many in- dustries are crippled, and others brought to a standstill, for lack of labour. No fewer than sixty-four centres have lately been established for the instruction of leisured women and girls in munition making, and it is hoped that large numbers will avail themselves of the opportu- nity of freeing the skilled workers who are so sorely needed in their ordinary work. The training offered is free, and the time required about six weeks. If we are to win the war the output of munitions must increase rather than diminish, and it will also depend largely upon women if we are to win the economic war- that is -sure to follow. The imme-:.iate need, how- ever, is for women who are not obliged to work for a living to take up the making of munitions.

A People's Theatre. ,The war, instead of paralysing country life, seems to have brought a great awakening. This is the result partly of agricultural prosperity, and of the establishment of .the huge Govern- ment factories, that have in many districts brought a large population of workers—mostly women—to sleepy villages. I know a village where there is a large munition works, and the workers having demanded recreation, a

"people's thearre" has been built, .Which Princess Christian recently opened. It accom- modates a thousand persons, has a.cafe without alcoholic drinks, and a stage for. fifty per- formers. It will be run on business lines, and the experiment will be watched with interest by outsiders interested in popular amusements. Taffetas for Autumn Wear.

The bunchy effects on skirts introduced last spring have failed to Emd lasting favour either with Parisiennes or Englishwomen, a sign that good taste has prevailed in both eases. These panniers had nothing but their novelty to , recommend them, for they were ugly in the ex- treme, whilst the straight skirt that has re- placed them is graceful, and displays the ' slender line that has new been worn for euite long time. The vogue for. taffetas led to their introduction, but there is no prosnect at pres- ent of any diminution in the popularity of that attractive and universally worn silk. On the contrary, variations of it appear every few weeks; the latest bid for favour is quite a novelty, for it is ribbed. It is quite sure of a warm welcome, and will be seen in the autumn in a wide range of colours. Satin and poult

OPINIONS ON THE WAR.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,— Your letters on the war have been well written, and are, on the whole, intelligent and interesting. There is a variety of opinion among my young people, but all agree that we are now winning, and in hoping that the war will soon be over. That is what we are all thinking and hoping at the present time. The Prize this week goes to

753 Niece Fanny Osborne, 31, LeiCester-street, who attends Evelyn-street School. She has won the prize previously. Well, a month soon passes—especially when it is a holiday month—and you will be back at school again on Monday. So for next week I will ask you to send me a letter on My Return to School and Lessons. That should be a popular subject, and all my nephews and nieces should take part in this par- ticular competition.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
Your loving UNCLE JACK.

THE WINNING LETTER

By Niece Fanny Osborne, 31, Leicester-street; age 13; Evelyn-strcet School. What I Think of the War Now.

"Britain, Russia, France, Belgium and Servia, all on the side of the Allies, are trying to crush Germany, Austria and,Turkey, which, I hope, they will do in time. This war, I am sorry to say, has' been caused merely by one man, the Kaiser. He has caused so much un- happiness and misery in thousands of homes, especially in. poor Belgium. England could not stand by and see Belgium punished for nothing. I hope it will soon be over, for the list of casualties every week is dreadful. The poor soldiers at the Front, I am sure, are tired of it, but still they have to fight, and they make the best of it. England is not so upset with the fighting as they are on the Continent. We are very fortunate that we live on an island and have a large Navy to protect us. I hope that the war will come to an end very soon, and I

am sure many other people hope the same."

===== 0002601_19160819_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHLY COMMENDED.

Niece Emily Marshall, 183, Winwick-road (Silver-street School).—" I think that this. war is a very shameful war indeed. It is often said, 'England for the right; Germany for might.' The war is a disgrace to humanity. We are having the best of it now. We are having com- plete victories, but we were not before. The war that is raging now has only been caused through jealousy and the German nation not being satisfied with the land they had got. They thought if they prepared and got ready for war they could conquer England. But Eng- land got ready, too." Niece Florrie Tickle, 8, Orford-avenue (ma- moat), says the war is the biggest that has ever taken place. If we have the luck to win it will crush Germany for ever. The war has been a good thing for some and a bad one for most of us, because we have lost fathers, brothers and relations, and have not been able to live on the money we had before the war.

Nephew John W. Peers, 73, Lyngoes-lane (Wycliffe), thinks the, war is going on badly. Many soldiers and sailors are getting killed and wounded, and he hopes it will be over soon. Nephew Frank' Overend, 74, Lythgoes-lane (Hamilton-street School), thinks the brilliant successes of our well-equipped army has seri- ously checked the enemy's forces. Many people thought it doubtful whether we should win the war when they heard of the 4th South Lancs.' losses and the hostile air raids. Britain, its dominions, and its faithful Allies will win the war, with the help of God.

Niece Florrie Overend, 74, Lythgoes-lane, says the Germans want to rule over England, which we hope they never will do. Our Allies are get.. ting on all right now. Every man in England should enlist, and serve his King and country, to keep our enemies from destroying our lands. Niece Winnie Roberts, 6, Portland-street (Sil- ver-street School), certainly thinks the war will sooi come to an end. The Russian Army must be the most wonderful organisation; it has up to-now had the most successes, and it will I,e owing to them we shall have a most glorious victory. Nephew T. Roberts, 6, Portland-street (Silver- street School), says: "The Italians have had their biggest victory, elipturing Goriza, with thousands of prisoners, many powerful guns and much material of war. The leaders of our Army and the others say Victory is certain.' The Russians are having victories all along their line, and have made tremendous captures. Their Cossacks, famous for their daring, have made their name in this war. The British in Egypt captured many Turks—and the South Lancs. were in it."

Nephew. Fred Hathbone, 112, Algernon street

t(Parochial), is of opinion that the war is a very bad one. Many of our soldiers and sailors nave been killed and wounded. The war is the greatest that has ever been known. It will be over in a few months.

Nephew Thomas Crawford, 8, Portland-street (St. Alban's), thinks our brave soldiers and sailors will soon end the war. It will be over in another few months; then the soldiers and sailors will come home again. But some of them have been killed in battle. They did not fight only for the sake of winning the war, but for God, home, King and country. Nephew Willie Feeney, 44, Catherine-street (St. Alban's), says the Allied troops are doing fine work, and the Germans are being crushed like a traction engine crushes the stones in the roadway. If they continue it will soon be ended. The Russian troops are doing their bit to end this murderous strife.

Nephew Dennis Farrar, 46, Willis-street (Parochial), thinks the war has now got so terrible that we can never settle it with fight- ing. The war is getting a bit too costly, not only in money, but in men; it now seems nearer to an end than ever it has done before.

Nephew Ernest Sankey, 99, Kimberley-street (St. Barnabas').—" The most important thing is that Britain and her Allies will win; but I do not think for a moment that the war is yet at its worst. It will,. I think, last another /ear, because Kitchener called men up for 'three years' active service and the war has now been on two years. Shells, bombs and bayonets are winning the war, but we must not forget our brave boys in khaki and also in blue. The sailors are guarding our shores and our ships, and, as far as possible, starving our enqmies. I think our air scouts are playing a great part in this war, as they tell the gunners the exact range of the object they are firing at."

Niece Florence Seabury, 3, Frederick-street (Bolton Council School), thinks, by the news in the papers lately, that the war will soon be over and we will see our brave soldiers and sailors home once more. "Only for those wonderful Russians, I don't know what we would have done, the war would not have been so near to an end."

Niece Maud A. Lunny, 108 a, Bridge-street.— "We are getting on very nicely so far, and per- haps if we wait a little longer shall have the good news, telling us we have won again. The soldiers are fairly doing their bit pow, and I am to do mine on Wednesday and Thursday, selling flags for our Navy."

BUSY READING HIS BOOK. • Nephew Denis Farrar, 46, Willis-street, who won the prize last week, writes : "Dear Uncle Jack,—l thank you very much for the beautiful book you sent me. I was so pleased and it is such a beauty that I could not write my letter to you on Monday because I ;vas reading it so much.—From your loving nephew, DENIS FARRAR!'

NEW MEMBERS. 989 Mary Holbrook, 10, Orford-avenue; age 11; Beamont Council School.

THE RIPPLING RIVER OF RHYME. Arthur Lewis Tubbs. From the beautiful mountains of Make-Believe, Through the fruitful Valley of Dreams, Where the blossoming shore slopes lower and lower, And is lost in the tide that gleams, Flow the wonderful river that winds its way With a song that is all sublime, As it glimmers in glory and tells a sweet story— The rippling River of Rhyme.

Would you wander away on this wonderful stream And along on its surface glide? Then 'tie well that you know, if you're eager to go, That a poet must be your guide. He will take you into his golden boat, And, losing all trace pf time, You will sail in a dream on the slumberous stream— The rippling River of Rhyme.

0! the land of the lily, the realm of the rose, The theme of a thousand sighs; The birth of the blush when the blood is flush, The light of love in the eyes; The silvery veil of the mellow moon, The tints of the twilight time, Are over and under the waves of wonder That ripple the River of Rhyme.

0! boatman, set the shimmering sails, And let us journeying be; With a gladsome song we will glide along Through the Kingdom of Poesy, Till the I.iver reaches the region fair, In that beautiful, bohntiful clime, Where life is a boon, and the world's attune— At the end of the River of Rhyme.

WARRINGTON SUMMER CAMP.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
End Of A Glorious Month Down By _

The Sea.

The second batch of boys for the camp ar- rived on Saturday, August 12th. The boys, 160 in number, were under the charge of Mr. Hol- brook (superintendent) and Messrs. Groarke, Knowles, Hammond, Oxley, Turner and Weir. Saturday afternoon was spent on the shore, many boys paddling, while others spent their time in picking shells, &c. Sunday was a wet day, so the boys could not get far away from the camp grounds. However, the time was spent in •a very happy, although quiet, manner. Monday morning arrived with a gloomy sky, but a start was made for the hills. A quarter of a mile from camp, however, the clouds broke, and the distance back to camp was done almost in record time. During the afternoon the boys were taken in the sea, and it was surprising to see the number of swimmers among them. Tuesday turned out a wet day, so the boys were made as happy and comfortable as pos- sible in the camp grounds. Among the children were two brothers, twins, who are born humor- ists. The boys simply screamed with laughter at their sayings and their various antics. The sports were held on Wednesday, all the boys entering for the various races. Good prizes were collected from various ,quarters, and were distributed on Friday evening before leaving for home.

On Thursday the children were taken to Rhyl, and the whole day was spent there in glorious sunshine. The Marine Lake (the Ethyl equiva- lent of the South Shore of Blackpool) was visited, and the various places of amusement were visited at half the usual price. What a busy .hour or two the various stallholders had —boys tumbling over one another to get to the stall. On leaving the pleasure grounds each boy received a packet of sweets.

Friday, the returning day, was spent on the shore, and by the number of shells, crabs, be., collected, Warrington will be scattered with them for the next few days. The boys were taken in the sea to bathe before returning. Warrington was reached last night at 9.30, after a glorious holiday by the seaside.

FORTY-FIFTH ANNIVERSARY.

Local Burial Society A Very LIN Body.

A large gathering of meznbeis of the War- rington Burial Society met at the British Workmen Public House, Horsemarket-street, on Monday to celebrate their forty-fifth anniver- sary of the establishment of the society. The President (Mr. James T. Barton) occu- pied the chair and was supported by the Vice- President (Mr. John J. Baskerville), the Treasurer (Mr. James Parr), Trustees (Messrs. William Eckersley, James Gresty, and Joseph Adams), the Auditors' (Mr. John Haley and Mr. David Harris), and the General Secretary (Mr. Lawrence Weir). The Chairman congratulated the members on another year's successful working, and added that under the circumstances of war the flourishing condition of the society was wonder- ful and reflected great credit upon the manage- ment, officials and the collectors for showing an increase during such times. The gain for the year would most certainly have been more had it not been for the extra taxes imposed. He was pleased to see that they had a progres- sive committee who recognised that the best insurance policy was the article that would bring new business, and, of course, new busi- ness was the greatest factor in the success, of any insurance office. The management had undoubtedly one of the best penny policies in the insurance market.

The General Secretary, on presenting his annual report, showed that the funds of the society now amounted to £5,691 is. 7id., as compared with £5,458 2s. mid. the previous year, a gain of £232 18s. 9d. The numerical strength was 1,588, as against 1,560 the year previous. The report further showed that they had paid away in death claims £lOl, compared with £ll4 the previous year, and they had now invested in the Warrington Corporation and upon mortgage alone £5,215. Regarding the officers for the ensuing year, Mr. John J. Baskerville was unanimously elected president and Mr. William Barton vice- president. Mr. John Haley was re-elected auditor for the next two years, Mr. James T. Barton being elected a committeeman to fill a vacancy caused by the death of one of their members.

The remaining portion of the agenda con- tained matters relating to the general interest of the members, in which the following took part in the discussion: Messrs. John Haley, Thomas Miller, John Sephton, William Hall, William Eckersley, Joseph Adams, William Massey, William Barton, and others. A vote of thanks was accorded the retiring President (Mr. James T. Barton) for the man- ner in which he had conducted the business of the society during his term of office.

..... LINK WITH THE 18th CENTURY.

Soap Over 100 Years Old In Possession Of Messrs. A. & F. Pears, Ltd.

A great granddaughter of the original Andrew Pears, founder of the firm of A. and F. Pears, Ltd., has recently died, and in consequence there has come into the possession of Messrs. Pears a circular piece of soap, four inches in diameter and about one inch thick, which is one of the first cakes of Pears's Transparent Soap manufactured by Mr. Andrew Pears, the in- ventor, about the year 1789. This most inter- esting industrial souvenir has stamped upon it the words:—

A. PEARS the Original Inventor.

and has been preserved in the family of Pears from generation to generation. The lady above alluded to was the last member of the family to possess this unique cake of Pears's soap, and also sister of the late Mr. Andrew Pears, who for many years had the direction of the company's soap factories at Isleworth, and who, died in 1909. This is the soap that was sold from the old shop in Wells-street, off dxford-street, in Georgian days, and was advertised in the papers and magazines of the time, in form shown in the following reproduction of two of the original advertisements :

PEARS'S TRANSPARENT SOAP.

PERSONAL BEAUTY depends so much on the appearance and texture of the skin, that whatever contributes to protect it from injury, or to improve it, must be considered an object of importance to all who are solicitous to pos- sess the advantage, which Lord Chesterfield' denorainates "a letter of recommenation on all occasions"; and certainly the present and future ages must feel themselves indebted to the Inventor of the curious Chemical Process, by which Soap is separated from all the impure and noxious substances with which, in its crude state, it is invariably united; this refinement is manifested by its transparency and fragrance. Prepared and sold by A. Pears, at his Manu- factory, No. 55, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London. But observe, that wheresoever, or by whomsoever sold, it never can be genuine with- out the Inventor's signature (A. Pears).

Another Advertisement of the period reads as

follows:

GENUINE TRANSPARENT SOAP.—It is now clearly proved that no SOAP can be pure or fit to be applied to the human skin, until it has undergone the process of refinement, by which it is rendered transparent; and when. this process is properly conducted, it will admit of no further improvement, but may be con- sidered the acme or summit of perfection in this article. BIRTHS, MARRIAGES, DEATHS.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
NO POLITICS!

It will be difficult for the ordinary layman to get away from the idea that much of the hubbub that has been caused by the introduc- tion of measures to prolong the life of the present Parliament and to bring the register partially up to date has been brought about by that latent political "liveliness" that, professions notwithstanding, undoubtedly exists. This country wants no election at the present moment. Where there have been forced contests in constituencies; the voters have shown their sense of the conduct of the discontents by rejecting them with the great- est possible emphasis. ~This week there was an " independent " aspirant to Sir Edward Grey's seat at Berwick. We don't remember the nature of this independent's programme, but it was " agin " the Government. The Coalition candidate won by 3,173 votes, Sir Edward Grey's majority having been only 1;686. The Army and the Navy do not want an election. Our Allies do not 'want. an elec- tion. The only people who do want an elec- tion are those who want certain politicians out of their path, as they wished them out of their path before the war began. They will not be gratified. We shall go on with the war and those who have brought it, through incredible difficulties, to the present promising stage will be allowed to go on with their task free from the blighting influence, of internal turmoil. As an instalment, the House of Commons has decided to prolong its own life for seven months—that is, until April 30th, 1917. The Government asked for , eight months' extension; the irregular opposition said it should be less—anything down to four. The soven months is a compromise on the right side. Very naturally the mere mention of the,life of the Parliament has set the politicians of all kinds to work upon their own particular ideas as to the reform of the register. Mr. Asquith has introduced a Bill containing the simplest possible suggestions for revivifying the voters' list, which has become very much war- bedraggled. Provisions are put forward of the following character:— (1) The qualifying period is to be postponed from July 15th to November Ist. (2) Soldiers, sailors, and war workers who are on the existing register will be placed on the new register, whether their qualifying premises are the same or not.

(3) Persons who have left their homes through the necessities of war work or whose homes have been destroyed by hostile bombardment, are to have the same privilege. (4) All persons who were in course of qualifi- cation are to be allowed to resume their quali- fying period when it was broken into by reason of the war, and to be entitled to come on the new register when the qualifying period is com- plete.

Of course, this was an occasion for the in- troduction of ideas. The most feasible one is that the soldiers and 'sailors and munition workers should have a vote and be able to record it, even if there is an election before the end of the war. We shall all acknow- ledge that those who have saved the country must have a ruling voice in the government of the country; but to ask the military authorities in Europe, Asia and Africa, and the naval authorities everywhere between the two Poles to send along the votes of their men would be to ask the impossible—apart from the inadvisability of distracting the attention of the men from the very serious work upon which they are concentrating. Besides, if any alterations are made in our voting qualifications, the women will want to be considered in those alterations. Then come the rival schemes of adult suffrage, man- hood suffrage, and all the other -contested points in our patchwork registration laws.

It appears to be essential that there shall be no election during the war; that a tempo- rary register shall be made, in case of need; that any Parliament that is eleeted on that stop-gap and admittedly defective register shall be limited in duration; and that the end of the war shall see a grand united effort undertaken to make Parliament really repre- sentative of the people. The point for imme- diate consideration is that we cannot approach one of these questions without tackling them all, and that the controversies such a course would set in motion would be fatal to our successful prosecution of the war.

Northern Old-Time Cyclists' Rally.

In furthering a general meeting of the Old- lime Cyclists, the Liverpool M.C.V., Manches- ter M.C.V., Consols of the C.T.C., Chester C.C., Anfield 8.C., and Manchester Wheelers have made arrangements to hold an Old-time Cyclists' Rally on August 27th, at Daresbury, meeting at four o'clock. All cyclists of 20 years' standing and upwards are cordially invited. The profits made from badges and collections are to go to the Cyclist Prisoners of War-Fund. Further information can be had from Mr. Jas. Critchley, 45, Sankey-street; Warrington, dele- gate to Liverpool M.C.V. for W.C.C.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 19, 1916.

Warrington Leads the Way in Practical Appreciation of

Our Sailors.

CROWDED GROUNDS AND GROANING COFFERS.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
Photograph by Birtles.

Back Row :—SIR ARTHUR CR,OSFIELD, BART., LADY ,HARMOOD BANNER, MR. W. PETER RYLANDS.

The Mayoress's baby is in her nurse's arms in the Town Hall doorway.

It is estimated that the cheque which Admiral Jellicoe will finger as a result of Thursday's "Navy Day" in Bank Park will not be far short of £2,000. At noon yesterday the cash paid into the bank was £1,766 18s., including over £7OO subscriptions. Fourteen thousand five hundred people paid at the gates, in addi- tion to those who passed through with tickets sold before.

officers and men of both services. Perhaps those in the most dangerous- positions at all times are the officers and men' in the engine room departments, but no danger will stop them performing their duty with that courage traditional in all British seamen. Without the help Of the Mercantile Mtiritie we cannot win this war. May all good Inek attend you and the chivalrous-minded people of Warrington. Yours ever, BERESFORD, Admiral." • (Cheers.) A wire was also received from Mr. Holman Kingdon wishing success. • . Mr. Harold Smith went on to say that it was with the greatest regret that Lady Greenall had found it impossible to come to open the fete. Those-Who knew her indornitehle•energy .would understand that nothing but the best of reasons would have prevented her .from -coming. They knew the arduous work she was performing • in France. The committee bad dione the right and only thing fn. asking ,His Workltip the Mayor to perform the opening ceremony i (Cheers.) They all knew the eneir,y and-• *thusiasm which Alderman Peacock had throwninto all the many duties which his position involved.. He had sacrificed' anything and everything in the ,inter- eats of that fete, and they all owed him a great debt. (Applause.) . The Mayor, -in declaring. %oldie 'open, asked those present to spend all the money in their pockets, and if they wanted a guarantee let them come to him, and, if he considered them. suitable, he would back them up to any amount they would like to' spend. (Laughter.) He men- tioned that they had already -received in cash from .the firms and people of Warrington a total of £620 up to noon that day. They endeavoured to get £lOO by the sale of flagthe previous day. They had received £l2O in cash, .and there was more to come in. (Cheers.) A number of tickets had also been. Sold, and he hoped to have a great cheque to send to Admiral j ell icoe for our brave lads of the sea.

Thus," Navy Day" was an immense success. With clouds hovering over in the afternoon Bank Park looked sufficiently commodious, but as fear of rain was dispelled, and as the people were loosed from the confines of their avocations the crowds assumed such gargantuan propor- tions that Hyde Park would have been better fitted as the venue. Never has the Park held so large .a mass of humanity before. The whole of Warrington left its homes for the fete, and those who left the town went into the country in fete motor cars.

_ The scene on the front lawn was unique and picturesque. Its beautifully decorated fancy stalls suggested an Emporium; two sheep and a sucking pig, with a bow of red ribbon on his tail, was a little corner of the Royal Show, and the manifold hampers of vegetables and fruit transferred one to a Wednesday morning market scene. The whole gamut of the wants of man- kind was covered, for in procuring so many articles for sale the ladies had been singularly fortunate. - - - -

Everyone was keen on taking a share in the enjoyment and success of the fete. Round and round the lawn marched "The Whitecross Band" (wounded soldiers from the hospital of that name), playing on penny drums and similar- priced whistles their tribute to the Navy with the tune "Red, White and Blue." Round and round the lawn also went a flower-bedecked donkey and cart (owned by Councillor Henry Roberts), from which Miss Barbara Johnson and Miss Joan Fairclough sold flowers, after they had given bouquets to Mrs. Harold Smith and Mrs. Rylands at the opening ceremony. Every- one was splendid. The Band of the South Lan- cashire Regiment; conducted by Mr. Cahill, played delightful selections, patriotic and fanciful. Behind the Town Hall Mrs. W. B. Roberts's orchestra and local artistes, besides those from the Royal Court Theatre and the Palace and Hippodrome, enchanted patrons, and in another part of the park all the fun of the fair—hoop-la, swing-boats, cocoanut shies, shooting, &c., &c.—were carried on with the usual vigour and sport. Mr. Birch Browne and friends were especially conspicuous with a side- show of Chinese magicians. Stentorian appeals for support, through a gramophone horn used as a megaphone, swept hundreds into their tent of illusion and good humour. Especially in. the fair were members of the V.T.C. and Special Constabulary, assisting in the management of the stalls and the crowds. In the evening there was a six-miles walking match, the prizes being awarded to: 1, J. T. Mulhall ; 2, L. Green; 3, Davies; 4, A. Mulhall; and consolation, J. Percival; and further concerts were held. The military band (the one conducted by Mr. Cahill and not the burlesque one) played for indulgence in what Milton called "the light fantastic" and unaffected mortals dancing. Hundreds and hun- dreds danced at a penny a number.

AEROPLANE AT OPENING CEREMONY.

It was a remarkably pleasing coincidence that as the opening ceremony, conducted by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), in the un- avoidable absence of Lady Greenall, was con- cluding, an aeroplane should pass over the Park on its way South. It seemed to beam good wishes for success upon the undertaking. The Star liinema photographer got a long reel of it, as also of the other features of the show. Among those present at the opening ceremony were the Mayoress, Sir Arthur and Lady Cros- field, Mr. and Mrs. Harold Smith; Lieutenant- Colonels Moggridge, Simpson, . Egerton Fair- dough and Dutton Fairclough.

Mr. Harold Smith's Distinguished Correspondents.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., who wore the uni- form of a lieutenant in the R.N.R., presided, and prefaced his remarks by reading a tele- gram from the First Lord of the Admiralty and a letter from Lord Beresford. Mr. Balfour wired:—

"Wish every success to your great fete. I feel assured that the 'citizens of Warrington will show their pride and confidence in the great Navy, on whose valour and seamanship the future of freedom and civilisation so greatly depends.—AßTHUß JAMES Buxom." (Cheers.) Lord Beresford wrote : "Dear Harold Smith,—lt is most generous of you and your friends at Warrington to have a Navy Day in order to send a big cheque for the Grand Fleet. I heartily wish you every success, and I hope your fete will run into four figures. It is difficult for people hi in- land towns to realise what the Navy has done for the Empire and our Allies during this war. Every day and every night acts of brilliant heroism are performed by officers and men•of

the Royal Navy and Mercantile Marine. The creepers, sweepers/ and patrol boats are in hourly danger of being blown to pieces, but nothing daunts the ardour and energy of the

===== 0002601_19160819_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
Sir Arthur Crosfield Eulogises Workers.

In proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor, Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart., said it gave him heart-felt pleasure to associate himself with what had been so admirably said by Mr. Smith about his Worship. Nothing could have been better put than what had just fallen from Mr. Smith. The Mayor deserved it all. His career in office had been Marked by unselfish and sincere devotion to public duty, and especially during the terrible time vt e were passing through. They all recognised the high-minded impartiality with which he had carried out his duties, without the slightest attempt to show favouritism to anybody of any colour. That was as it should be in our generation at any time, but above all in the great time we were passing through now. It wds for that reason that he (Sir Arthur) felt special satisfaction in listening to the most appropriate remarks that fell from our Member on the matter. "May I also associate myself with the real expression of regret at the circumstances which have prevented Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall being present?" added Sir Arthur. "We should all like to have seen them with us. (Hear, hear.) I happen to know something of the practical way which, with great liberality and absolutely ceaseless energy and devotion to the work, Lady Greenall has been carrying out this really fine work she is doing on the Continent. We are sorry she cannot be with us,. but wish her God-speed in carrying on that work when she returns to France. (Cheers.) "I now want to congratulate the ladies in charge of those stalls which are going to lead to a successful end to Navy Day. In an infinity of directions ladies have responded to the call of the country at this great time. (Cheers.) I should like to couple with these few words of recognition our sense of the great services ren- dered by the Secretary (Mrs. W. Peter Rylands) on this occasion, because for weeks past, morning, noon and night, she has not ceased to labour for the success of this under- taking. (Hear, hear.) I hope it will be a great success. The Mayor has given you the straight tip." (Laughter and cheers.) Alluding to the work of the Navy, Sir Arthur remarked that there was no end to its variety and no limit to the magnitude of its importance. While new standards of valour and new tests of endurance well* being established in. a thousand cases in different parts of Europe, 'in this great struggle, it was, difficult to gingle out instances, but they all knew that the success of the landing of our Lancashire lads at Cape Helles Was greatly helped by the men of the sea with deeds ' which would go down in history for all time. They might ransack the records of all history, and they would not find a feat of arms more marvellous and heroic than that of the Lanca- shire lads, aided by the Navy, in Gallipoli. (Cheers.) •Let them that day. bear in mind such deeds as those, and might they add zest to the success and pleasantness of the fete that after- noon and evening. (Cheers.) Mr. W. Peter Rylands seconded. The Mayor, in reply, took occasion to refer to the way in which Mrs. W. Peter Rylands (the hon. secretary) had worked incessantly for the success of the fete, and also heartily thanked the ladies who had given their assist- ance. Councillor Roberts propoied three-times-three for Mr. and Mrs. Harold Smith. Their Member was a man of untiring energy; of great ability,.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STALLS.

The fete was formally opened at 2.30. An hour earlier the stallholders—eager and un- trammelled—had started business and got into a promising trade stride. The pretty lawn in front of our Town Hall will want considerable attention for some time: Ceaseless movements of hundreds of zealous workers and equally anxious purchasers have left their impressions on the pleasant sward, but what matters it so long as " Warrington leads the way"? The stalls were arranged after the fashion of an 'old English fair, and much taste and skill were displayed in their decorations. Occupying, very properly, the most . effective position in the centre of tfie lawn was the charming Plant, Flower and Fruit Stall, a blaze of colour, where a large band •of helpers assisted the ladies in charge—Mrs. Rylands, Mrs. Ralph Johnson, and Mrs. Brereton 'Fairclough. Low- ering skies and threatening 'showers had not the slightest effect upon the numbers who thronged about tle Ice Cream o Lemonade and American Drink Stall, where an enormous quantity of these "refreshers" was disposed of. Near by, the Cake Stall, crammed with alluring confectionery, was speedily ravished of much of its charms; while, the Fancy Goods Stall, in charge of Lady Harmood-Banner, Mrs. Frank Cooper and Mrs. T. Pemberton, had a bewildering display of dainty articles and bric-a-brac. The Pound Stall was a capital idea, worked by Mrs. Osborne and her staff. It's really wonderful what a pound of anything or everything means. The variety was specially impressive when one remembered the motto chosen for the stall: "Don't speak of what you are going to spend—spend it." The Glass and China Stall was a marvel of attrac- tiveness, and, vulgarly speaking, knocked the Town Market into fits; while the' Sweets Stall spoke for itself. The lines attached to the latter display were scarcely apropos—" Brother, there's work before us: work for our native land." It was not work: it was pure, unadul- terated pleasure. The Dairy Stall was heaped with produce and the amount of good fare to be disposed of may be judged from the fact that Mrs. Bower, Mrs. J. W. Hayward, Mrs. F. Houghton, Mrs. E. Marsh and Mrs. W. A. Savage were assisted by no less than twenty one lady helpeks. Special interest naturally centred in the Hat Stall in charge of Lady Crosfield, who was ably assisted by Miss M. Broadbent, Miss P. Johnson and Miss Walker. This stall may be described as a little bit of Bond-street in Bank Park. These dainty specimens of the milliner's art were either Parisian models or trimmed in the very latest style. Adjoining the stall was a space enclosed with Union Jacks, where all the mysteries of "trying-on" were performed by lovely votaries of fashion. One stall was an exceptional centre of attraction to an eager crowd of pros- pective purchasers—three-deep and more com- ing up. It was the Second-hand Stall, with a big collection of articles of wearing apparel hanging up and lying down. This collection was cleared somewhat early in the afternoon, and it is worthy of mention that most of the stalls were emptied of their contents , before six o'clock. Mr. John Mainwaring, who had kindly consented to act as auctioneer in dis- posing of all unsold goods, had a light task. Many housewives looked longingly at an enor- mous block of coal—about 6cwt. It advertised the announcement of the sale of 50 tons of coal by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd.

The Lottery Department, in charge of the Mayoress (Mrs. P. Peacock) and Mrs. Harold Smith, assisted by Miss S. Taylor and Miss E. Jackson, was busy all day, and tickets for numerous draws were bought up eagerly— wounded soldiers disposed of 'a large amount. It should be mentioned that price lists were displayed at most of the various stalls—a most laudable feature of the Navy Fete—and al. though prices ruled low, returns were high, and the result must have proved most gratify- ing financially. An efficient staff of the War- rington Cycling Club and friends worked in the cash department, under Mr. George H. Smith, and Penny Tickets were the order of the day. These tickets only were accepted in payment at the various stalls and amuse- ments. Some reckless people bought them by the roll. The Cloak Room and Parsele Officca very useful adjunct—was in charge of Captain J. B. Starkey, assisted by the officers and the mem;

hers of the Latchford Cadet Corp, with Miss Starkey as cashier. Officials and Stallholders.

THANKS TO THE PEOPLE.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—We desire to take the earliest oppor- tunity of expressing to the people of Warring- ton our very great appreciation of the splendid response to our appeal on behalf of Admiral Jellicoe's Grand Fleet Fund for Wounded and Disabled Sailors.

While we cannot attempt to render adequate acknowledgment of the services rendered, and the money spent so willingly; we would like to assure the workers on the one hand and the ,Igenerous public on .the other that their com- bined efforts have surpassed the most extrava- gant hopes" Of the promoters. To the large band of ladies and gentlemen who voluntarily rendered such loyal and de- voted service we an never hope to express our appreciation of their untiring energy and enthusiasm'. We hope and believe that the great.success.of the fete will prove a not inade-' quate reward for all they have dcine.. To the general public who so ungrudgingly and so freely spent their money in this deserv- ing cause we would say that their generosity was worthy of this cause and worthy of our town, and we would remind them that every penny spent at the till will go to aid and com- fort some gallant sailor vdio has cheerfully endured suffering and pain for the country and the flag that he loves.—We are, yours, &c., . HAROLD SMITH, President. PETER PEACOCK, • Chairman and Hon. Treasurer. NORA RYLANDS, Eon. Becretitry.

Infectious Disease. There are 54 local cases in hospital —Scarlet fever, 24; enteric, 1; diphtheria, 10; phthisis, 19. New cases during the week :—Tuberculosis, 6; measles, 7; diphtheria, ,scarlatina and enteric, one each.

Chamber of Commerce Essay Prizes. The Chamber of Commerce prizes offered to the scholars of the Bateler Grammar School for, the best essays on "The Duties of 'Citizenship' have been won by L. J. Witts in the Upper and J. H. Chidlaw in the Lower School.

Flour realised 455. to 495. per 2401 b., 2s. 2d. to 2s. sd. per 201 b. being the price of thirds. The price of bran and other offals was is. 6d. to Is. Bd. per 201 b., and wheat strim 70s. per ton.

Judge Scott Fox Leaves Warrington. It is officially announced that his Honour Judge Scott Fox, 8.C., will, as from the Ist October, be transferred from the Birkenhead County Court Circuit (which includes War" rington) to Circuit No. 14, in place of his Honour Judge Greenhow, resigned, and. that Mr. Barnard Latley, K.C. will, .as from the same date, be appointed judge of the Counii Courts on Circuit No. 7, in place of his Honour Judge Scott Fox.

Thirty-eight Births. In the week ended August 12th there were in Warrington 38 births, against 40 in the corresponding week last year.' The death 9 numbered only 13, against 20 last year. The births were 17 males and 21 females- The deaths arose from the following causes Two each from phthisis and premature birth; one each from diarrhea, enteritis, tubercular' disease, cancer, pneumonia, and heart disease; and three from all other causes.
