Scouts v. Cadets. On Monday evening two Boy Scouts of the Ist Latchford Troop (Christ Church), engaged upon scout practice, played a smart manceuvn by capturing the colours from the centre 01, the camping ground of the Latchford Cade' Corps at Bradshaw Farm. The alarm given" by two Cadets returning to camp caused a. stern chase, but the Scouts, who showed clever tactics, arrived home with the colours. If the captain of the Cadets will kindly communic.ate with Scoutmaster T. A. Edwards, 17, street, between the hours of 6.30 and 7 13' Tuesday night, the 22nd August, he will re- ceive back the colours which were taken 11 his two Scouts.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 19, 191 G.

PRIVATE A. HOUGHTON.

Shot Through Heart While Firing Machine Gun.

A gallant story is attached to the death of Private Albert Houghton, a young fellow of 21 years, of 20, Priestley-street, who was killed in action during the morning Of the 9th inst. "During the 'attack," his officer has written to Houghton's widowed mother, "he, with the other Lewis gunners, stuck to their gun and did their whole duty well. He was shot through the heart and died, immediately, suffering no pain. . . . He was well likod by everyone." What better tribute could a Soldier earn?

Previous to being called pp on the outbreak of war, Private Houghton worked as a moulder at the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., and went to the Front early last .year. His father, who died eighteen years ago, will be remem- bered as the band sergeant i:t the Old Volun-

t' s

teers. Houghton received his education at St. Barnabas' School, and was: member of the choir in the church adjoining.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE F. CHANDLER.

Killed While Bringing In A

Wounded Officer.

An old soldier, in the person of Private Frank Chandler, of the South Lancashire Regiment, : brother to Mr. William H. Chandler, 151,-. Orford-lane, fell in action on 'July 3rd whilst carrying in a wounded officer. A man of the same battalion, writing to a comrade in hospital, said: "Private Chandler got hit in the leg and then was carrying a wounded officer in when a shell burst between him and three more, killing all of them." Private Chandler, who joined the Army when he was eighteen .years of age, served in India and EgYpt and throughout the South African campaign. Some years ago be was employed by Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., but afterwards went to reside .at Seedley, Manches- ter, where his widow and two children new

DIED OF WOUNDS.

PRIVATE W. PARSONAGE.

Seventeen-Year-Old Soldier Dies Of Wounds.

Only seventeen years of age, Private William Parsonage, of the South Lancashire Regiment, youngest on of Mrs. Parsonage, Fernlegh, Orford, has laid down his life for his country. A week last Wednesday a letter was received from a comrade, Private J. Povey, of Orford, who said that Private Parsonage had been wounded. Hearing nothing further, Mrs. Par- sonage wrote to the authorities and was grieved to receive on Tuesday morning the •following communication from the Records Office :—" It is my painful duty to inform you that a report has this day been received from the War Office notifying the death of Private W. Parsonage, of the South Lancashire Regiment, which occurred in France on the 3rd August. The cause of death was 'Died of wounds received In action.'"

Private Parsonage, whose only brother,

Fusiliers, has died of wounds in hospital in Rouen. • About three weeks ago his uncle, Mr. .1. T. Massey, 149, Longford-street, Warrington, was first informed that he had been seriously wounded with shrapnel in the head and • arm, and on Tuesday last a, note was received ..from the matron stating that he had died on. the 28th July. ••

Privates Noon went to Echool in Orford-lane,

land subsequently worked at Manchester. His mother has been dead for some years, and his father, William Noon, is also serving in the Lancashire Fusiliers. This young fellow ' had been eight months in the Army before war broke out and ..he- went. to France with the first batch of the Lancashire Fusiliers. He was wounded in the arm in the retreat of Mons and was kept in France until he got' better. He was back in the firing line at Ypres and got a bullet in his right thigh. ' Having recovered from this second . wound he was given five or six weeks' leave and came home. He then went to his depot, from which , place he was sent out to Salonika. There he received his third wound—shrapnel in his left leg. He got well in Malta, but poor Noon was too valuable a man to part with after escaping thus far in the whole campaign. He was ordered back to the trenches in France, and this time r&Tived his death wound—pro- bably in the great advance early in July.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
DIED FOR THEIR eOUNT "Y.

This Week's Very Heavy List of Local

Casualties.

/...CE.-CPL. N. MILLINGTON.

Former Staff-Sergeant in The Church Lads' Brigade.

Unofficial .news has :reached his widow at 144, Church-street, of the death in action of Lance- Corporal Nathaniel • Millington, of the machine gun section •of the South Lancashire Regiment, and an enthusiastic mem- ber of the St. Clement's Company of the Church Lads' Brigade, in which company he held the rank of staff-sergeant. Writing. to Mrs. Milling- ton under date August 10th, Sergt. J. Crookes said: "I am so sorry to have to tell you the painful news about your husband, who was killed instantly two days ago. Whenever you think of him, please remember that he died a good soldier's death (for he was a good soldier, if ever one lived); and for a good cause. I, being the sergeant of the Lewis gunners, could always rely on things going on all right if Lance-Cor- poral Millington was in charge. As far as I can gather the particulars as ,regards his death, he was in 'No Man's Land and had fired all the ammunition which they had in the gun at the time. He was coming back and handing the gun into the trench when he fell a victim to one of the enemy's snipers, who shot him right through the head. He was a good man, and I, along with all the Lewis gunners, send you our deepest sympathy, and hope you will not take the sad news too hard."

Lance-Corporal Millington, who was 28 years of age, and the father of one child—a little girl —was formerly employed as an iron moulder at the Grappenhall works of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd. He was educated at the National Schools, Church-street, and wor- shipped at St. Clement's Mission. He joined the Army soon after the outbreak of war, and had been at the Front eighteen months. Two

-I

more brothers—Harold and William—are serv- ing in the Army.

KILLED.

CAPTAIN KENNETH WRAY.

Articled To A Warrington Solicitor.

• Information has been received that Captain Renneth Wray, who was articled to Mr. Herbert Hatton, solicitor, of Messrs. Robert Davies and Co., Market-street, has been killed in action. Re was in the South Lancashire Regiment, and Was a son of the Rev. G. W. Wray, vicar of Tim,perley. He was twenty-five years of age, and was gazetted second-lieutenant in Septem-• ber, 1914, going to France in February, 1915. In the autumn of last year he was promoted to the rank of captain.

Died Bravely And As A Soldier.

have lost—Captains Wray and Collingwood, Your son, Second-Lieutenant Mills, Second- Lieutenant Lawford—since the night of the Bth, and Second-Lieutenant . SECOND-LIEUT. LANCELOT SMITH.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Wounded at Hooge.

Lieut. Smith, who had a splendid physique, Spent his twenty-fourth birthday recently in the trenches. He joined the South Lancashires the Christmas before war broke out, and fought through the earlier stages. On the 16th June, 1915, he was wounded in the leg at Hooge, Ypres salient. Lieut. Smith was a private when he volunteered for active service when war -broke out, but had attained the rank of corporal at the time he received his wound. In recognition of his bravery and splendid qualities he was gazetted second-lieutenant on the 19th Decem- ber, 1915. He only went out again to France so recently as the 13th June of the present year. lie was educated at Warrington Grammar School, and was subsequently articled to Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall and Co., accountants, Bewsey-street.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Grappenhall Bellringer.

lie was one of the bellringers at Grappenhall Church, and was so interested in this delightful hobby that while in training at Tunbridge Wells he assisted the ringers there on many occasions. He was also a splendid swimmer. When he received his commission a large num- ber of friends expressed their admiration at a happy gathering in the Winmarleigh Cafe and Presented him with a beautiful cigarette case.

LANCE-CORPL. W. HALLAM

A Former Local Hairdresser Killed In Action.

Lance-Corporal William Hallam, of the Royal Fusiliers, a well-known local hairdresser, was killed in action on June Bth. His parents, Mr. and Mrs. William Hallam, reside at Fen- ton, Staffs, but Lance-Corporal Hallam made his home for seven or eight years with Mrs. James, The Poplars, Lovely-lane. Lance-Corporal Hallam, who was 28 years of age, was employed fo; many years by Mr. Birch Browne, hairdresser, Sankey-street. He joined the Army on September 4th, 1914, and had been at the Front for some time. He is the second son of Mr. and Mrs. Hallam to lose his life in the war, his brother, John, having been killed about twelve months ago.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLEMAN H. BRIMELOW.

Rescued From Burial To Be Killed Later.

A few weeks ago Rifleman Horace Brimelow, of the King's Royal Rifles; sent word to, his par- ents, Mr. and Mrs. G. L. Brimelow, of Man- chester-road, that he had been buried for an hour and a half with a sergeant and other men, but after some yeoman work by comrades was rescued. This week, however, the sad news has come that he has since been killed in action. Rifleman Brimelow enlisted in January of this year and went to the Front in May. Since join- ing up he had only one leave, and just before going to France his father, who is chief draughtsman for Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., went to his training quarters to spend a few days with him. His letters from the Front have always been of a cheerful nature. On July 30th he wrote: "Up to now I have gone on very well, and if the same luck follows me it will be. all .right. There is plenty to See. It is not all hard work; in fact, in the trenches at times it is very slow. I am sure when it is all over it will be very nice to say I did my bit." The Rifleman was attached to the bombers.

photograph' and a short time ago sent along the of himself and five of his colleagues in this sec- tion. They looked a happy and cheerful com-

pany. • . He was ecognised as being a first-class shot, and acted asn..sniper on many occasions. Before enlisting he was in the employ of Messrs. Henry Milling and Co., Ltd., with whom he had been since ite left Arpley-street School.

MPIAJIM

CORPORAL G. W. HERBERT. Killed.

battery have had a very heavy month, and it got worse and worse as time passed on. It got harder every day to bring up ammunition to the gun. The night before last your son was driv- ing up when the Germans started to shell us very heavily. Your son drove up very gallantly and kept his horses quiet until the ammunition was unloaded.. Just when he was getting away to safety a shell burst, just beside him. He was killed instantly, along with his two horses. He was buried next day in Caterpillar Valley, where he is with many other gallant men like himself."

Driver Phibbs, who attained his twentieth birthday in June, joined the Army two years ago, and had been in France fourteen months. He was formerly employed at Messrs. Cunning- ham's, brewers, and later at the Gas Works.

PRIVATE H. CRAVEN.

Step-Father And Four Brothers Still Serving.

Private Henry Craven, of the Manchester Regiment, who was killed in action on July 30th, was one of the five soldier-sons of Mrs. G. Worthington, 21, Warburton-street, off Win- wick-road. 'His step-father, Private George Worthington, is also a soldier, having seen ser- vice at the Front, with the Warrington Terri- torials. He is now on duty at the Drill Hall. Thc four remaining sons are :—Thomas Henry, the eldest, of the South Lancashire Regiment; William, of the South Lancashire Regiment; James, of the South Lancashire Regiment; and Edward, of the Manchester Regiment. Private Henry Craven, who was twenty years of age, was formerly employed at Bewsey Forge. He enlisted in the K.O.R.L. Regiment soon after the outbreak of war, ',and had been at the Front ten months, being transferred re- cently to the Manchester Regiment. He was educated at Silver-street School and attended Silver-street Sunday School.

PRIVATE J. A. AS,FITON.

" Died Nobly Doing His Duty," Says Officer.

Unofficial news has reached Mrs. Ashton, 108, Lythgoes-lane, that her husband, Private Joseph Albert Ashton, of the South Lancashire Regiment, has been killed in action. The news was conveyed in the following letter from Capt. W. K. Armstrong, dated August 12th :—" I feel I must write you a few lines at this time of your great loss, if in doing so I can convey some slight comfort to you. Your husband was greatly respected in the regiment and he died nobly doing his duty, at the same time as the officer whom he serve 4 so well. It may re- lieve you to know thfa the end was swift and painless. Please accept the sympathy of the company officers and uself." Private Ashton, who; was formerly employed at Messrs. Rylands tros.' Works, joined the Army in September, 1914, and had been at the Front since January,.l9ls. He only went back to the Front about three weeks ago, having attended the funeral at Warrington of his father, Mr. James Ashton, who formerly re-

been in the Army exactly two years. He went out to France with the first batch of Terri- torials, and was wounded about twelve months ago, and had, only been back in France a few months. He was a member of the old Volun- teers for about fourteen years. He was formerly employed in the joinery department at the Che- shire Lines Railway Co.'s works, and.,was edu- cated at the Hamiltcwstreet School. -

LANCE-CORPL. J. SCOTT.

Another Brother And A D.C.M. Cousin Still Serving.

About three weeks ago Miss Scott, 41, Fitz- herbert-street, received a field card from her brother, Lance-Corporal J. Scott, East York- shire Regiment, informing her that he was wounded and had been admitted to a - base hospital. Nothing further was heard of him until Miss Scott received news that her brother had died from wounds. Lance-Corporal Scott, who joined the Army at the outbreak of war, and had been in France thirteen months, was 21 years of age. For nine years he was employed at Sankey Wire Mills, and was a member of St. Luke's Church and Bible Class. A brother is serving in the Army, while a cousin has been awarded the D.C.M. and French honours. Another cousin, who was taken prisoner, has been recently exchanged.

WOUNDED.

CORPORAL J. 11. KENT.

Last Of Four Patriotic Brothers IVounded.

Corporal J. H. Kent, of the South Lancashire Regiment, has been wounded in action. To this effect he has written to his wife, Mrs. Kent, 11, Selby-street, and adds that the wound is in the right leg. Corporal Kent, who is 22 years of age, for- merly worked for Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. is an old boy of Wycliffe School, and attended St. Paul's Church. He had three brothers in the Army; one has died of wounds and the other two have been wounded. One of the two has returned to duty.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL E. WYATT.

"'fed Bibby" Wounded For The Third

Time.

Mr. and Mrs. Bibby, of 22, Steel-street, have received news that their nephew, Lance-Cor- poral E. Wyatt—better known as Ted Bibby— has been wounded. On Sunday morning Mr. Bibby received a letter from him, saying he arrived at a Stockport Hospital. Mr. Bibby and his son took train to Manches- ter and then car to Stockport. When they ar- rived he had been sent to Withington. They then took car from Stockport to Withington, and found him in Withington Nell-lane Mili- tary Hospital. The following was% the letter received :—"I

have arrived -at this hospital, 2nd Western General Hospital, Hollywood Park, Stockport, near Manchester. I am a bed patient, right hand wound and left forearm and both knees. I was hit by a German tortoise bomb. I came down the Seine on a Red Cross ship and across the Channel to Southampton. I was hit on the 6th August, between 6 o'clock and 7 in the morn- ing. I think I shall have finished my fighting. career for a while, so someone else can have a go."_ _

When 18 years of age Lance-Corporal Wyatt tried three times to enlist in a line regiment at Orford Barracks, but was told he was rather small, and that if he came again they would have him locked up. This, however, did not deter him, for he walked to Bury and joined the Fusiliers. He served four years with them, and then joined the South Lancashire Regiment at Warrington and completed three years with them. When war was declared in August he was lodging at Huskisson, near Liverpool, work- ing for the Cheshire Lines Committee. He came home next morning and joined his old battalion again. In May, 1915, he was wounded in the right

hand an -again in the great charge on June 16 was wounded in. the back and kept in. the base hospital. Rouen, until September, 1915. He attended Wycliffe day school and Brick- street Mission Sunday school.

Private Aaron Lawless, of the South Lanca- shire Regiment, son of Mrs. Longworth, 13, Prince-street, Latchford, has lost his right arm in action. Twice Wounded—Once By

Cupid's Dart.

Leading Seaman and Gunlayer H. Penning- ton, R.N., who was recently wounded in the naval fight, celebrated his -recovery by return- ing to his native town on Saturday last, to his mother's residence, 149, Church-street. His short leave expired on Thursday night, but our gallant sailor friend and his bride had an opportunity of enjoying Warring- ton's Navy Day. Leading Seaman Pennington formerly worked

for Mr. Hill, chemig, Buttermarket-street, and has been fifteen years in the Navy.

Member Of Athletic Club Wounded In

Left Arm.

Hughes, 329, Liver- pool-road, a member of the Warrington Board of Guardians, was wounded on July *2Bth. Fletcher, Rue- sell and Co., Ltd., where he had, worked since he was 13 years of age. He ran with the War- rington Athletic Club's team which secured honours in the Northern Senior Championship

in 1913, and formerly played football for the Warrington White Star team. He is an old boy of the Arpley-street School.

Twenty Yards. Nirs. Wareing, 28, Wellington-street, has re- ceived a letter from her husband, Private Arthur Wareing, of the South Lancashire Regi- ment, now in-hospital at Stourbridge, who in- forms her that he was wounded in the recent fighting. He writes as follows: "I got blown about twenty yards and fell on my back, and afterwards I got some shrapnel in my hand, but it is not so serious. I am not sorry to be in. England again after being in the thick of it. They have advanced about four miles near - Wood, and have taken a village."

Private Wareing, who is 20 years of age, and a former moulder at the Grappenhall works of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, has been in the Army about ten months, and at the Front five weeks. He was educated at Arpley-street School.

Church Lads' Brigade Boy Wounded

In Two Places. Mr. Smart, confectioner, of Queen-street, who has three sons in the Army, viz., Rifleman H. J. Smart, Private Harold Smart, and Private Albert Smart, has heard that Private Albert Smart, Royal Welsh Fusiliers, has been wounded in the arm and side.

Private Albert Smart is at present in hos- pital at Petersborough.— He formerly attended St. Paul's Mission, being a bugler: in the Church Lads' Brigade,)ang was 'educated at Heathside School. His favourite sport was boxing, he . being very good at this particular branch of exercise. Of the other two brothers, Rifleman H. J. Smart has been wounded, but has re- turned to duty, while Private Harold •is still serving in France. .

GUNNER ARUM R BL

Progressing Favourably hi A French

Hospital:

Gunner Arthur Blain, of the Royal .Field Artillery, and son of Mr. and Mrs. David Blain, of 8, Windsor-street, received gunshot wounds in both legs on July 18th, and is now in a con- valescent camp at Rouen. His mother received a letter from him on Monday morning, in which he informed her that he was progressing favour- ably, although he was rather lame. Gunner Blain, whose only brother, James, was wounded in France while serving with the Territorials, is 25 years of age. He was formerly employed as a carter by the L. and 11.-W. Railway, has been in the Army twelve months, and at the Front eight months.

All's well that ends well!

The C,onneil began to water the roads!

By CRITIC. "Ye old. rushbearing!" Is the old custom completely dying out?

Even in this sore time of trouble our thoughts are apt to stray to the rollicking times our forefatheiv experienced in days gone by.

For " Rushbearing Week" was always the recognised holiday for Lymm.

Dancing round the old Cross, the whirl of the merry-go-rounds and all the fun of the fair were part and parcel of "Rushbearing Week."

Everyone made merry, And even the parsons were forced to work overtime.

As I walked round the historic Cross last Monday I could not help thinking what a marked contrast there is between ancient and modern times.

Nevertheless, several firms granted their employes the usual holidays.

, Owing to excursions being "barred," the Customary Blackpool trip was "off."

As regards my hint last week I was pleased to see that our tradespeople took the trouble to inform the public prior to closing their establishments last Monday.

Walking up the station hill the other day, I ran into quite a garrison of Lymm soldiers home on leave.

I hear that they are to have a surprise- packet before they return from their old "pals" who are forced to remain behind. .

What it is I am not in a position to say, but I hear that the local butcher and green- grocer are preparing for a "swell in trade."

I hear that a service is to he held to-morrow night at the Congregational Church to the memory of the late Private Tlaomas Read.

It will be remembered that this gallant soldier was killed during the opening stages of the big push.

The change in the weather last week-end was predicted in the usual way.

Walking through the village the other day I noticed a lady wearing a silk stocking on one leg and a woollen one on the other. Has she been studying how to keep cool, or did she oversleep?

Il k - 4 t The "war saving" scheme is doing well. _ Only this week I heard that a local fir ni of , munitioneers had opened a Savings' Associa- ,. b ,' ~ -1 ,1 ii, ,I c,,l', 11 tion amongst their employes for the purpose of obtaining war savings certificates.

LYIVIM IN MOURNING.

Funeral Of The Late Mr. A. W. Callender.

Lymm was in mourning on Saturday for one of its most prominent and widely-respected residents—Mr. Arthur William Callender, J.P.— who had passed away at his residence, West View House, early on the previous Thursday morning.

Public sympathy was shown on every side and Union Jacks were flown at half-mast on the Urban Council Offices, the Conservative Cleab, the Parish Church, and other places in the village. Long before the hour fixed for the interment a large number of people gathered in the graveyard, which occupies such a strik- ing situation overlooking the beautiful I,ake. The funeral procession itself was of a unique character. As it started from the de- ceased gentleman's late residence the bell in the Parish Church began to toll in solemn cadence, and the roadway was lined with sym- pathising onlookers. Up to a certain point the members of the local Fire Brigade, under Captain William Rose, headed the procession, but later the Urban Council occu- pied this position. In the cortege also marched members of the Lymm Tradesmen's Association and various officials of the Conservative Regis- tration Association. Mr. Harry Pearson and Mr. F. E. Evans, as churchwardens, carried their staffs and were accompanied by other church officials. Then came the hearse, fol- lowed, by the chief mourners ad a number of sorrowing friends. At the entrance to the churchyard the re- mains were received by the Rev. G. B. Thurston (rector) and the surpliced choir. The coffin was then borne into the church and placed on trestles in front of the chancel.

The service opened with the singing of the hymn "On the Resurrection Morning," after which Psalm xc. was chanted. The Rector read the lesson and intoped the prayers, the service being choral. The hymn "Ten thousand times ten thousand" having been sung, the coffin was carried from the church, Chopin's Funeral March being played by Mr.. W. B. Cooke, the organist. The committal prayers at the grave- side were read by the Rector, and the choir chanted the "Nunc Dimittis," after which the singing of the hymn "As now the sun's declin- ing rays at eventide descend" appropriately ended a solemn and impressive service.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====

The massive coffin was of 'English oak, with a cross in relief the full length of the lid. The coffin was wrapped in a rich purple velvet pall, with heavy gold fringe. The grave was lined with mosses and ferns, interspersed with lilies and other choice white flowers.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM MAGISTRATES' EXPRESSION

OF SYMPATHY.

At Lymra Sessions on Wednesday, Vice-Chan- cellor D. Stewart Smith (who was in the chair) said: Before we proceed with the business of the Court this morning it is our sad duty to have to refer to the death of our friend and colleague on this Bench, Mr. Callender. For nearly forty years he lived in Lymm, and dur- ing the whole of that time he identified himself with every organisation or institution having for its object the welfare of those among whom he lived. Coming as he did from a very distin- guished family, some of whom in the past had rendered important services to the State, I know as an old friend of his that it was always his wish to do. good where he could, and he did I it. And so when a few years ago he was ap- pointed a magistrate of this country it was universally felt that his public services and also his character fully justified that appointment. We regret that we shall see him no more. Many of us have,been his intimate friends for a long number of years, and it only remains for us to offer to Mrs. Callender and her family our most sincere sympathy in their bereavement. Mr. H. R. Harris (clerk to the Court), in join- ing in the expression of condolence,' said they could not but deplore the loss of such a public- spirited man as Mr. Callender.

DEATH OF MRS. CAROLINE WRIGHT.

It is with profound regret that we announce the death of Mrs. Caroline Wright, which took place at her residence, Meadowside, on Satur- day morning, in her 70th year. Mrs. Wright was exceedingly well known in Lymm and district, being the widow of the late Mr. J. W. Wright, head of the firm of Messrs. William Wright and Sons, gold- beaters and refiners, of Newfields and late •of Manchester. In religious circles she was a prominent Baptist and attended the Cherry- lane Baptist Chapel, where she was, as she was generally, highly esteemed. She had been in indifferent health for some time and had recently undergone two operations, and wa& apparently doing well until Saturday morning, when she had a relapse and passed away.

The Funeral.

The funeral, which took place at the Parish Church on Tuesday afternoon, was attended by a large number of relatives, friends and employee, and the service was conducted by the Rev. G. B. Thurston. The coffin was carried into church by four local gentlemen— Messrs. T. Knowles, W. H. Moston, E. Edwards and F. Guest—and the funeral service was read by the Rev. G. B. Thurston, a large congrega- tion being present. The principal mourners were: Messrs. Ernest Wright, Harold Wright and J. W. Wright (sons), Mrs. Gibbon and. Mrs. Perry (daughters), Mr. William C. Sum- mers and Mrs. Summers (nephew and niece), Mr. J. Gibbon, Mrs. J. W. Wright, Mrs. H. Wright and Mrs. E. Wright (son-in-law and daughters-in-law), Mrs. Clark, Mrs. Egerton and Miss Wright (sisters-in-taw), Miss Evelyn Wright (niece), Miss Gladys Wright (grand- daughter), Mr. and Mrs. Frank Wright (bro- ther and sister-in-law), Mr. Charnley (Preston), Mr. Broomfield (Warrington), and Mr. Thomas Smith (Halifax), intimate friends.

In addition to those sent by the family, the following wreaths were received :—Dr. Henry Wright, the Employes of William Wright and Sons, Ltd.; Mr. and Mrs. Hills, Mr. and Mrs. Sharpies, Mrs. Cash, Mr. Charles Blease and family, Mr. and Mrs. Schofield, and Miss Annie Vogt.

The funeral arrangements were carried out by Mr. W. G. Groves, Booth's Hill.

EXCESS' FARES.

Warrington AVorkmen's Extra Ride. At the Lymm Sessions on Wedneeday, two Warrington workmen were prose- cuted by the L. and N.-W. Railway- Company for travelling between Heatley and Lymm rail- way stdtions on the 23rd and 24th June with- out paying excess fare.

-Bra-nthwaite, solicitor, Manchester, for the company, said defendants were both work- men living at Warrington, and had weekly workmen's tickets to Lymm. Heatley, where they were employed at the Salt Union Works, is one station farther on from Lymm, and although they paid excess fare in the morning when alighting at Heatley, they returned to Warrington on the evenings of the dates ma- toned without taking extra tickets. Evidence having been given by several rail- way officials, both defendants expressed regret at what had occurred, and they were each fined 20s. and ordered to pay 10s. special costs. Joins "Suicide Club" and meets some Local Pals.—Private Harold Wibberley, of the Che- shire Regiment, who prior to the war resided at Statham, sends the following letter home: have just heard from a pal that our Tom is wounded, so please write and let me know how he is going on. I have got transferred to an- other battalion, and have landed in the same company as Percy Appleton, Herbert Thomason and Wm. Read (all Lymm lads). Will you please send me some writing paper and 'fags.' Don't bother sending any money because in some places we go we cannot change it. We are now out of the trenches for a rest, and I am with the machine gunners ('Suicide Club '). It is a grand job, I can tell you, for you cannot half mow them down." Private Harold Wib- berley was educated at Lynam School, and wor- shipped at the Primitive Methodist Church. The Tom referred to is his brother, whose por- trait we published as wounded last week.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 19, 1916.

PECULIAR CHARGE.

Hotelkeeper's Wife Fined For Being Drunk.

An unttsual charge was preferred at Lymm Sessions on Wednesday against a publican's wife, when Mrs. Mary Emma Forsyth was prosecuted for being found drunk on the licensed premises of Edward Forsyth, the Mill- stone Hotel, Lymm, at 2.55 p.m. on the 24th July. Supt. Sutherland prosecuted and Mr. Field- house, of Manchester, defended. P.C. Atherton said he had occasion to go into the Millstone Hotel, having seen a lady going in with a little child, and passing through to the backyard. He saw Mrs. Forsyth in the corridor of the hotel. She was staggering and holding herself up by the wall; her hair was down over her face, and she looked very strange in her manner. She followed him into a room on the licensed premises, and kept banging herself at the doorways. He could see plainly that she was 'drunk. There .were a man and woman at the bar and six females in the taproom—they were jigging while the gramophone was going. He went to the Police Station and reported the matter, and returned to the hotel with Sergt. Davies and Sergt. Griffiths. They saw Mr. For- syth and went into. a room at the back. The licensee's wife came staggering in from another room, and caught hold of the table to keep her- self up. Sergt. Davies said to Mr. Forsyth: "Your wife is drunk," and she said, "I am very sorry," and spoke in a broken voice with difficulty. Mr. Forsyth said he had just got up, and did not know she was like this. The Ser- geant advised him to get her off the licensed Premises, and he said he would get her to bed. He also said he would close the house at nine o'clock, when he went to work at the Steel Works.

Witness having stated to Mr. Fieldhouse that this happened at 2.55 p.m., and that the house was closed for the sale of intoxicants at 2.30, the latter submitted that he had no case to answer. When the premises were closed for the sale of intoxicating liquor they ceased to be licensed premises, and no person could be charged for being drunk on them. The contention was disallowed.

In further cross-examination, Constable Atherton said he did not make any accusation against Mrs. Forsyth at the time he, first entered the hotel. He did not see defendant- making up any sandwiches for the picnic party. Sergt. Davies gave corroborative evidence as to Mrs. Forsyth's condition. He asked Mr. For- syth had his wife been in charge of the prem- ises, and he said she had. He added, "I did not know she was like this. Had you been a few minutes earlier you would have heard the expression, 'What have you been taking.'" By Mr. Fieldhouse : We visited the. house to satisfy ourselves that everything was all right and in order. He knew defendant's husband was away on munition work at night. Acting-Sergeant Griffiths gave similar evi- dence.

Mr. Fieldhouse said the husband, who sleeps in the earlier part of the day, did nothing to evade responsibility. Was it conceivable, if the police case was as stated, that they would have let the case rest there, and would not have had the landlord before the on" nq well? The Chairman: You don't object to that?

Mr. Fieldhouse replied No, but he used it to test the accuracy of the evidence. It would be stated by Mr. Forsyth that the defendant had taken orders for drinks and food for' this picnic party, and that his wife came from the smoke. room and transferred portion of the order fOr minerals and Oxo to•him. She went into the kitchen to prepare the food and make up some sandwiches.

Mrs. Forsyth described how she took the orders from a picnic party which came to the hotel at 2.50. They had only non-intoxicants and Oxo and some " Beero "—also a non-in- toxicant—and ordered bread and cheese besides. She went into the kitchen to prepare the Oxo and make the sandwiches. When Sergt. Davies said to her, "You are drunk," she said, "Am I? Thank you." She did not hear what he said to her husband.

By the Chairman: She did not suggest to the Sergeant that if he thought she was drunk she would see a doctor. It was untrue to suggest that she was drunk. .

Mr. Forsyth gave evidence in corroboration, but admitted to the Superintendent that when the Sergeant said his wife was drunk, he re- plied, "She seems to have had some." The Chairman said the magistrates thought the ease proved and defendant would be fined ten shillings.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
BOATING ON THE LAKE.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Can you spare .me a little space just to say a word in regard to boating on the lake at Lymm? I should like to ask: Does the lake belong only to the few, or to the general public of Lymm? I remember a few years back, when the buying of the Waterworks and lake was under consideration, and the Council members were holding their public meetings, to explain how matters stood, to the people of Lymm, before going to the poll on the question—that at the meeting held in the schools,'Pepper-street— a question was asked to this effect: "Would boating be allowed?" The answer given then by one of our Councillors was, "If so, it would be for private boating." Till then I had always thought he was a working man's friend. Why are the working classes left out of this? Let those who have enough and to spare of this world's goods have their private boats; but also let the working classes have a chance of a little pleasure a well. Then, again, would it not pay, looking at it from a business standpoint? Some, perhaps, will say this is not the time to think of such a thing. Then why, in the name of goodness, should the few have that pleasure while the war is on? Let us remember that the men from the cottages,. as well as from the homes of the rich, have left home and friends, and are fighting side by side out yonder, for the safety of England, to keep such beauty spots as our dear old village of Lymm from harm and danger. Perhaps the members of our Council will think the matter over, and remember that the working man and his family enjoy boating.

ONE- WHO HAS TRIED TO DO HIS BIT. St. Margaret's Camp, Heaton Park.

Local Success at Colwyn Bay Show.—A t; the Colwyn Bay Poultry Show, held during last week, Mr. W. Jeeves, Hartfel House, Reddish, a well-known local poultry farmer, succeeded in carrying off a first prize with a Columbian Wyandotte cockerel, and got highly commended for another exhibit. He was the only com- petitor from Lymm.

LANCE-CORPL. W. HYNCH.

Popular Statham Soldier Dies

Of Wounds.

Lc.-Corp. Wm. Hynch, of the Manchester Regi- ment, reported last week, as woanded, has suc- cumbed to his wounds. A telegram from a base hospital to his wife, who resides at Statham, announced the death, and the news was received throughout the village with profound regret, for the gallant soldier was a very popular gentleman, and had a large circle of friends. He is son-in-law to Councillor Frederick Lockyer, our worthy Council chairman. He is one of a splendidly patriotic family, for out of four brothers, he has died, one has been a prisoner of war since the memorable retreat from Mons, and two are at present serving in France. He has also two brothers-in-law serving with the Colours. Shortly after the outbreak of war he volunteered for service and joined, the Man- chester Regiment. He was drafted out to France about ten months ago, where he had some very exciting experiences. He was edu- cated at Manchester and worshipped at the Lymm Parish Church. In civil life he was a warehouseman, and was employed by R. Goodain, of Moseley-street, Manchester, for whom be had served for a period of over 17 years. In sporting circles he was widely known,

STOCKTON HEATH.

LANCE-CORPORAL J. W. MARSH.

First Believed Killed, But Only Wounded.

On the 10th inst. Mr. and Mrs. Marsh, of 5, Whitefield-road, Stockton Heath, were startled by the receipt of a letter from an officer in the South Lancashire Regiment, stating that their son, Lance-Corporal Joseph William Marsh, was missing and believed to be killed and expressing his sympathy. But quickly following this came a field post card from the young man himself, saying that he had been wounded. Greatly relieved by this news their minds were completely set at rest on Wednes- day morning by receiving a letter from their son, in which he says:—" I hope you received my field post card which I sent two days ago stating that I was wounded. Don't worry about anything; it is not so bad. I was lucky to get out of it as lightly as I did." He is _wounded in the leg, and wrote from Boulogne. Lance-Corporal Marsh is only 22 years of age and went out in- February, 1915. He was edu- cated at St. Thomas' Parochial School, Stock-

PRIVATE ARTHUR EVANS.

Relatives' Dramatic Intimation Of His Condition.

On Monday morning Mrs. Brown, 1, Cawdor- street, Stockton Heath, was surprised by the visit of a railwayman, who handed her a dirty postcard. There was a hole in the end of the card, in which a leather bootlace was tied, and this had also been fastened round a wad of newspaper. The message had been found on the railway line. The card ran: "7.30/13/8. I am passing through this station in the ambu- lance train on my way to hospital at Aberdeen." The message was from her nephew, Private Arthur Evans, South Lancashire Regiment. The card had been thrown out of the Scottish ex- press as it dashed through Warrington Station. Later Mrs. Brown got a letter, which had been written on board a hospital ship. "Once again," he wrote, "I am on my way to 'Blighty.' Now don't worry; my wound is not serious. We were advancing and I got a dum- dum bullet through the muscle of my left arm. Cheer up. lam in the pink. Will let you know

===== 0002601_19160819_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
SAPPER THOMAS E. LLOYD:

Suffering From Gas Poisoning For The Past Fortnight.

We learn from Mrs. Lloyd, 24, Hawthorn- road, Stockton Heath, that her son, Sapper Thomas Edward Lloyd, R.E., has been suffering from gas poisoning for the past fortnight. He was brought back to England, and is now in hospital in Newcastle-on-Tyne. Sapper Lloyd is 26 years of age, and previous to joining the Royal Engineers in September, 1914, was employed in Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Company's works as a moulder. He was married in June twelve months ago—Mrs. Lloyd is now in Gosport—and was sent out to France in the following month.

CIIESIIIRE VOLUNTEER REGIMENT.

Stockton Heath Detachment.

Orders for week commencing 20th August :

Sunday.—Church Parade; fall-irt Stafford- road, 9.30; assemble, Town Hall, 9.45; recruits to attend in civilian dress with brassards, where issued.

Monday.—Company Drill, Headquarters, 7.45; Recruits' Squad, Headquartors, 7.45. Tuesday.—Appleton Difle Range. "Class Firing" for all members who have nc,t taken it this year. The following recruits to attend at 7 o'clock for instructions: S. Bailey. J. Jackson, F. Hatton, H. L. Clark', R. W. D. Cook and R. J. Moss.

Wednesday.--Appleton Rifle Range Squad Practice for " Service " Competition. Thursday.--Recruits' Squad, Headquarters, 7.45; Signalling Class, Headquarters, 7.45. The Recruit Squad is open to " exempted " and " badged " men. Enrolments Monday or Thursday evenings. NorE.—N.C.O.'s must see to rifle i being out at each drill.

FRANK WARBURTON, Officer Commanding.

PAD GATE.

Matriculation Success.—At the recent matri- culation examination of Manchester University, Miss Gertrude Clarke, eldest daughter of ex- P.S. Clarke, was placed in the first division. She commences as a student teacher in the Pad- gate C.E. Schools on October Ist.

Wesleyan Church Service on the Lawn.—Our Wesleyan friends made an agreeable change from the usual church service on Sunday even- ing. Instead of being held in the church, the service was, by permission kindly given by Mrs. Thomas Sennett, of Bruche Heath, held on the lawn. There was plenty of accommodation for the large congregation which assembled, and the hearty singing of tho4e present helped to make the service a memorable one. The Rev. A. J. Morton preached.•

Funeral of Former Padgate Policeman.—On Sunday morning Mr. 'Heath, a police pensioner, residing in Padgate, died from stroke. He was born in Nantwich in June, 1864, and first joined the Lancashire police force, being stationed at Tuebrook, in the West Derby Division, where he remained for five years, transferring after- wards to Knotty Ash. On the extension of the Liverpool city boundary, he transferred to Pad- gate, remaining there till he retired in Septem- ber, 1910. When the war broke out he re-joined as a special constable, but was later employed as constable by the C.L.C.—The funeral, which took place at Christ Church, Padgate, on Thurs- day, was conducted by the Bev. J. Hirst. The hymns sung included "Rock of Ages" and "Days and Moments," and the organist (Mr. F. Blackwell) played the Dead March in "Saul" at the conclusion of the service. The chief mourners present were: ,Mr. W. Heath (son), Mr. T. Heath (brother), John (son-in-law), Mr. Leach, Mr. J. Roberts, ex-P.S. Clarke, ex-P.C. Clough, Mr. Sherlock, Mr. Haddock and Mr. Edwards (stationmaster), representing the

THE R.A.0.8.

Four Brothers Raised To The Second One Of Four Brothers Serving In The Degree. Army.

All the pomp and ceremony attached to the ritual of the Royal Ancient Order of Buffaloes were displayed on Monday evening at the Fleece Hotel, the headquarters of the Lymm Lodge, in connection with the raising of four of its brethren to the second degree. The large Lodge Room in which the ceremony was performed presented a pretty spectacle, the the walls being decked with portraits of high officials of the Order, and in one corner, pro- jecting with stately pride, hung a magnificent pair of horns, suitably inscribed, the gift of Bro. Carse. The ceremony was performed by the members of the Grand Council, who had

journeyed from Manchester for the occasion and consisted of the following brethren: P.G.A. H. Mitchell, C.P.; G. Shields, K.0.M.; A. E. Ingle, K.0.M.; H. Bobbins, K.0.M.; T.P.G.P. Charles F. Wilson, C.P.; ,G.P.R. John Morrow, K.0.M.; and P.S.A.j. Charles Gardner, K.O.M. The brethren/raised to the high degree were: Bros. Sam Webberley, T. Vost, W. Lawrinson and J. Tnornley. Tea and Toasts. Prior to the ceremony the visitors were enter- tained to tea by the host, Bro. J. Thornley. Toasts were then given for "Absent Brethren," "The Lymm Lodge," and the "New Brothers," and, at the request of the P.G.P., the whole audience rose and bowed heads, as a token of respect to the memory of Bro. Scholes„ C.P.. P.G.C., who, since their last visit to Lymm, had passed away. At regular intervals during the evening, when the Lodge was not at business, harmony was rendered and some singing of. very high vocal merit was obtained.

A large number of visitors were present, re- presenting Lodges from various provinces. They included: Bros. J. W. Craig, "Garrick" Lodge; C. A. Archer, K.0.M., "Lonsdale" Lodge; A. E. Jones, D. Danson, C.P., "Queen Alexandra" Lodge, Warrington; A. Lowe, K.O.M. K.G.H., "Duke of Lancaster" Lodge; J. Chadwick, "Torn Sangster" Lodge; W. Chadwick, Sam Craig, R.0.H.P.P.G.P.; Primo Pearcy, "Lonsdale" Lodge; E. Creasey, "Long- dale" Lodge; Primo Grounds, who officiated at the piano; and William Thompson, C.P.. "Mersey" Lodge, Widnes. Altogether there were upwards of thirty members of high order present. At the conclusion of the evening Primo Pearcy, being the oldest Primo of the Lymm Lodge, moved a vote of thanks to the members of the Grand Council for so willingly coming down and performing the ceremony; and to Bro. J. Thornley, the worthy host, for supply- ing them with tea. This was seconded by Primo Atherton, the youngest Primo of the Lodge, and confirmed by Primo Burns and carried unanimously.

Past Old Resident's Bowling Feat.—A very unusual bowling feat has been accomplished at Leigh by a gentleman aged 73 years, who re- sided at Statham 40 years ago. In a handicap competition, in which he played a point behind scratch, he won a silver cup and special prize of two guineas. On the donor of the latter hearing that so old a competitor had been suc- cessful he readily doubled the amount. Mr. Blain is well-known in Lymm, having several relatives in the district, among whom are L's brcther, Mr. Thomas Blain, of Statham. and brothc-r-in-law, Mr. Albert Leather, of Booth's Hill Post Office.

Mr. an'd Mrs. Edward Goodyear, Heath-street, Stockton Heath, have been informed that their son, Private Edward Goodyear, South Lancs. Regiment, has been wounded in the right arm, and was then (Sunday) receiving treatment in a base hospital. He is 36 years of age, and joined up last year, going out to France five months ago. He attended St. Thomas' Parish Church, Stockton Heath, and had been employed by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. There are four brothers of this splendid family—all volunteers—serving in the Army. Three are in the South Lancashire Regiment— Edward, just mentioned; Thomas (32), and Ben-

jamin (25), the latter being at present in Alder- shot. Thomas, who is at Yarmouth, has not recovered from serious wounds received in his left arm some months ago, and Joseph (20), who is in the Army Service Corps, served in Salonica and is lying ill in Malta.

His father and mother (peculiar coincidence) are buried in Aberdeen, where he has been taken to hospital, and he lived with his aunt, Mrs. Brown, in Stockton Heath, for the past eight years. He was educated at People's College, Warrington, and served his time to the gentlemen's outfitting with Mr. Banks, Horsemarket-street. PRIVATE WILLIAM P. LAKE.

Struck In An Ankle And Now At Aberdeen.

Mrs. Welsby, 51, Walton-road, Stockton Heath, sister of Private William Podmore Lake, has learned that he has been wounded in the ankle and has been sent to Aberdeen. The postcard received by Mrs. Lake, Parker-street, Warrington, only read; "Wounded. On way to Aberdeen."

Private Lake, who is 34, had been in France for eighteen months. He was on furlough last November, and took the opportunity of getting married. He has had many escapes from death, having been in sortie of the bloodiest engage- ments. Private Lake belonged to Trinity parish in' his youth, and was educated at People's College. He previously worked for Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Company.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
HILL CLIFFE BAPTIST GIRLS' GUILD.

On Thursday of last week a sale of work was held in the schoolroom. The work stall was well filled with articles made by the girls at their meetings. Miss L. Grounds presided at .the opening ceremony, which was performed by Mrs. Henry Roberts, who, in an able speech, urged the necessity for the girls becoming good needlewomen, and the advantage it would be to them in after life. A spray of orchids was then presented to Mrs. Roberts by Miss Phyllis Moss, one of the Guilders. The girls, under the leadership of Mrs. Roughsedge, sang "Red Cross Nurses," "Six Little Marks" and "Three Flags." The drill displays were under the leadership of Miss Dodds. The children's per- formances were greatly appreciated and heartily applauded. Miss Elsie Brocklehurst gave a pianoforte solo, and Miss Byfield accompanied the children on the piano. The room had been tastefully decorated for the occasion. The work stall was presided over by *Mrs. Curtis and Mrs. J. Moss; flower stall, Mrs. Wilde and Miss Byfleld ; refreshment stall, Mrs. Williams and Mrs. Brocklehurst ; tea room, Misses Dodds, Grounds, Simpson and Mrs. Ault. Numerous games were entered into by the young people, and the net profits, which are to be divided between the Church and Guild, amounted to about ..t2O.

War Relief Society:—The Padgate War Relief Society have just issued a balance sheet slid lag the accounts from August, 1914, to 3001 June, 1916, and a careful perusal shows that this village has responded nobly to the numerous calls made upon it in order that our soldiers and sailors may be provided with -com- forts. The total. income from subscriptions, concerts, • collections, &c., amounts to £164 9s. 5d., and the expenditure £153 14s. na., ing a balance in hand of £lO 14s. 9-id. The ex- penditure has been chiefly upon materials, which have been made up into useful articles for the soldiers, and the committee are to be congratulated on the large number of article 9 which have beien. distributed. The article made and sent away from August, 1914, to 30th June, 1916, are as follows: 300 belts, 248 shirts, 267 pairs mittens, 502 pairs socks, 260 mufflers, 52 pairs knee caps, 60 helmets, 54 flannel vests, 36 pillow cases, and 48 face cloths, sent to the Lord Derby Hospital; 36 bed jackets to the Red Cross Hospital; 69 bags to Lady Smith Dorrien; making a total of 1,932 articles.

MEN WANTED.

Tribunals To Help In Keeping Up Continuous Supply.

At the meeting of the Warrington District Tribunal on Wednesday, Mr. William Furness, military representa- tive, said that a meeting of military represen- tatives was held at Liverpool last week_ Dur- ing the course of that meeting a statement wae, made which he thought he would be justified in making to the Tribunal. The War Office require so many thousand men a month_ The men are wanted now more than three months hence. They are anxious to keep this plPh going, and if the Germans were not kept going it would mean a handicap in the future. Thel asked the Tribunal to share the responsibilitY of enabling the military representatives to get the men they required. Another matter which they might consider was the advisability of making cases at the present time final, as the military wanted to know what men were going forward and what men were available. TheY should avoid adjournments if they possibly' could. Since the meeting an order had also come out which altered the condition of things of some weeks ago. They had a tentative arrangement by which the military representa- tive could have a man put on munitions if he had special responsibilities. That was entirell done away with, and no man would be allovred to go on munitions except in exchange for a young unmarried man. Every man was wanted that they could possibly get into the Arn39: Another point was that all men, no matter, what their age, up to 41, were to be considered eligible—age should have no consideration with the Tribunal. He thought it only fair to bring these matters before them, as he knew that, as a Tribunal, they were anxious to help the military. The Chairman (Mr. George Mather) said gr. Furness was right to tell them candidly that they, as a Tribunal, ought to be in possession of these facts.

X 182,000,000 IL YEAR ON DRINK.

The following has been issued : The National War Savings Committee calls 4ttention to the fact that the sum now being 14%1t,by the nation on alcoholic. liquors is esti- 'ea at £182,000,000 a year, and appeals d'hillestly for n immediate and substantial re- 11;etinn of this expenditure in view of the d gent and increasing need for economy in all "eDartinents of the nation's life. It is entirely outside the province of a corn- Initt kajtee appointed to deal with war savings to anY pronouncement on the social and cold"eal questions involved in the limitation ohv.nontrol of the sale of 'alcoholic liquors, but iii szlil°ll6l3' in the present national emergency a ,„ ott- exPenditure of practically "..500,000 a day th sPirits, wines and beer cannot be justified on the ground of necessity. This expenditure. szetefore, like every other form and degree of t4ll'enditure beyond what is required to main- to health and efficiency, is directly injurious ,national interest!. te'Much of the money spent on alcohol could the/saved. Even more important would he (1) qtl4 Saving for more useful ,purposes of large (2) ntities of barley, rice, maize and sugar, and the setting free of much labour urgently

needed to meet the requirements of the Navy and the Army. _ _

"To do without comforts and luxuries and all else that is not essential to health and efficiency while the war lasts is the truest patriotism."

"THE TRADE" REPLY.

The National Trade Defence Association issue the following:—

(1) Out or the total of about £182,000,000 a year at least £68,000,000 goes in the pocket of the State; not a bad share for "the sleeping partner." The amount contributed in some cases comes to over three-fourths of the net pro- fits of the brewer.

(2) The duties on spirit, beer, wine, and licences have risen from in 1913-14 about £43,000,000 to at least .t 65,009.000 for 1915-16. (3) An Act is in existence reducing the output of beer by 15 per cent. of that of 1915-16, or 30 per cent. of that of 1913-14. (4) It was an ex-Chancellor of the Exchequer and an ex-Minister of Munitions who informed the public in November, 1914, "that every half- pint that a man drinks lie will be contributing to the carrying on of the war."

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS.

Colonel Arthur Stanleigh and Judge Lamar Stan- :l6W'. cousins. of Virginia, are bitter enemies over the possession of The Diamond from the Sky, a inassive jewel heirloom found centuries ago in a f•allen meteor. Colonel Stanleigh, when a daughter ja born, buys the male heir of a gipsy king, that he raay keep the jewel. He plans to rear Esther. the rl, in secrecy: Esther and The Diamond„frorn the Sky are stolen by Hagar, the gipsy mother. Years later Hagar returns Esther and the diamond, but of the old group only Dr. Lee is living. Blair Stanleigh kills Dr. Lee, and steals the jewel. Then Blair Stanleigh and Arthur Stanleigh, the false heir, fight a duel. Arthur, thinking he has killed his cousin, flees in a racing motor-car. In his mad flight the machine goes over a river bank, and that night the moon shines on a body floating down the stream. Arthur is found by Esther and Hagar and nursed to health. Hagar tells him his real iden- tity. Arthur swears to make himself worthy of Esther, and leaves, taking the diamond, and meets Blair Stanleigh. Blair takes him to a great ball, Where they meet Vivian Marston, a New York belle, wearing the diamond. Hagar is there, too, telling fortunes. Vivian has just seated herself When a brawny forearri clutches The Diamond from the Sky. A greater confusion than that caused by the loss of the diamond arises when the Sheriff of Pairfas arrives at the ball and demands the arrest Of Arthur Stanleigh. He escapes, partly through the efforts of Hagar, and later finds work and refuge as a farm hand. Luke Lovell drops the diamond into a mail bag. The gem clings by its clasp to a rent in the bag, and is afterwards found bY Quabba, the hunchback organ-grinder. He does It°t retain it long; Clarence, his monkey, drops it 111.,an owl's nest. Arthur, working on a farm as " dam Powell.," masks himself and goes to the Fairfax tournament, where he wins the highest honour. He is betrayed by Blair Stanleigh, and tatlid great 'confusion the young Virginians fight to uar Arthur's escape without avail. Hagar, holding Proofs of Blair Stanleigh's guilt, is brutally at- tacked by the young man and her mind destroyed. The diamond finds its way to a Chinese opium den. Whence Blair steals it. Vivian 'marries him, and they escape from Tom Blake in a train, which t.olibers board and take the diamond with other Jewels and money. Pursued by a Sheriff's posse, the, train robbers lose the diamond and treasure, the latter 'being found by "John Powell." Blair Dresses his attentions upon Esther, who spurns his brofessed love. Luke Lovell finds the diamond and loses it. Then it is found by an Indian squaw.

Despairing of ever finding Arthur Stanleigh, the fugitive Earl, Marmaduke Smythe resolved to re- turn to England. Upon the eve of his departure he received official notice of the sale of Stanleigh Hall and its contents. The lawyer coveted the deer's head, one of the hall's most attractive orna- ments, and resolved to bid for that and take it back to England as the souvenir of a land of strange people. The same day Mrs. Lamar Stanleigh re- ceived a similar letter.

" Blair," she said, "the hall is for sale. It will go for a song. I'll buy it, and at last Stanleigh Hall will belong to us."

Other plans were being formed for the purchase of the hall. Tom Blake had kept Arthur informed, and on the morning of the sale he, received a tele- gram. "Thanks tor information. Bid up to twenty thousand dollars for Stanleigh Hall. Safe- guard Esther's interests. Keep everything secret.— John Powell."

Blake rushed from Richmond io Fairfax aid reached the hall in time to whisper to the auc- tioneer that he was commissioned by a client to bid. Thus the widow found the detective ready to bid when the sale opened. As a special favour Mar- maduke had been allowed to purchase the deer's head, and leaving immediately had missed the great scene of the day. Bidding started low. Mrs. Stanleigh. not intend- ing to pay a big sum, offered " Five thousand." "Sin," responded Blake. "For whom is he bidding?" whispered Blair. "I don't know. Seven,"*came from his moth?r.

Up mounted the bidding until at fifteen thousand Mrs. Stanleigh became alarmed, but " Seventeen " came from the detective.

"Blair, I cannot go beyond seventeen-five." " Well, try him at that. It will probably ei..ttle him," said Blair. But " Twenty " was Blake's response, and fle hall and its contents passed to his client, and the widow and her son left the • scene not troubling to . disguise their chagrin.

If before his well had proved a "gusher" "John Powell" was in a position to send Tom Blake twenty thousand dollars for the purchase of Esther's homer his new wealth placed no limits upon his expenditure. To celebrate the occasion he gave a dinner to a select circle of friends. He entertained them to the best that money could buy, and the dinner was one of the most notable Santa Barbara had witnessed.

One of the diners in the hotel was very curious concerning the identity of the young 2nan of whom such a fuss was being made.

_ It was Vivian Marston. In funds through the soft-hcartedness of Abe Bloom, she was on the look-out for a guy, and that evening was dining at the expense of an elderly admirer. "Who is that young man?" she asked the waiter. "Oh, he is John Powell, the young oil king. He is giving a dinner to celebrate a 'big snoop." "Thank you," replied Vivian to the man, men- tally noting that a young oil king was worth keep- ing an eye on. Meanwhile Esther and Quabba were with the gipsies. The girl was determined to spare no effort to find Arthur. Now that Hagar bad been pro- vided for, her mind was relieved. Probably it would be some time before she would be well enough to be removed, and the weeks could well be spent in seeking the lost yo-ang man. Marmaduke Smythe had abandoned the quest, but that was no reason Why she should do the same. To the gipsies she said: "I am called far away to seek a friend. Hagar will soon be well, and I will rseturn with her to you." Then the girl and Quabba set out upon a trail which would lead them —where?

Accustomed to the rough knocks a hard, unfeel- ing world dealt those who disregarded the straight ways of life. Luke Lovell was not long out of IL money-making proposition. He allied himself with an illicit whisky pedlar, and one day the pair found themselves at a hut which an Indian and his squaw had selected as a temporary home. Around the neck of the baby hung The Diamond from the Sky. Luke made a grab for the jewel, but the squaw was too quick for him. She plainly indicated that the pretty ornament was not for sale, and neither of the men had anything suffi- ciently attractive to barter.

Then a bright idea entered Luke's head. If the father of the infant had the average Indian's love for fire-water, they could make a deal. The man was quite willing, and the diamond changed hands for a bottle of whisky of doubtful character. Luke and his pal made quickly away, while the unfortun- ate husband, taking a long drink from the bottle, saw it dashed from his hands by his indignant squaw. Not only had he lost the spirit, he also had the misfortune to endure a vigorous thumping. There came a day when the two men reached the hills overlooking Santa Barbara. Walled by the green high hills and the grey and higher mountains at the back, beautiful Santa Bar- bara sloped down to the sea. The fronds of stabely palms waved in the mild evening air: The vivid scarlet of the flowers and the emerald green of fair lawns beautified the scenes below. Fair as the "Gardens of the Gods" seemed, the scene to the desert-burned eyes of Luke LoveTl and his partner, as they stood upon the hill near the old mission, was as a panorama of paradise. Far off At anchor on the sapphire waters of the bay a great white yacht rode like a swan upon the tide. " A guy rich enough to own a skiff like that can afford to buy the diamond from us," growled Luke Lovell. And in the oncoming dusk they plodded down the hill. That night they untied a boat at the water's edge, and Luke Lovell pulled upon the oars to find them a purchaser for the diamond. But the evil men do who fall beneath the great jewel's baleful spell was not yet at an end. Luke's companion rose stealthily behind him and struck him down. There was a hoarse cry in the night, a death struggle on the water. The boat went over. a drowning man gasped in agony, and The Diamond from the Sky sank in the deep, cold waters of the bay.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 19, 1916.

CAPT. W. P. RYLANDS.

Major Fraser Visits Warrington

Volunteers.

OFFICERS APPOINTED.

Apart from having received a fillip through the influx of conditionally-exempted men, an- other step forward has been achieved, by the Warrington Company of Volunteers, Major Fraser, who is in charge of the 7th Lancashire Battalion, to which the Warrington Company is attached, having formally appointed Coro- mandant W. P. Rylands as Captain, and Sergeants W. Ashton, F. V. L. Mathias, C. H. Hoult and H. Davies as officers.

This fact was made known by the Major at a parade for the purpose of enrolment on Sun- day morning. Major Fraser, along with seventy men of the Headquarters' Company and a bugle band, journeyed by motor transport from Liverpool in order to make an inspection and assist in the enrolment.. They all lined up on the lawn in Bank Park, the number of local men on parade being 149. After inspecting the men, the Major expressed his delight at their appearance on this his first visit to the Warrington Company. He hoped they would all pull together and that every man would do his utmost to become efficient and worthy of membership of the 7th Battalion Lancashire Regiment. He was hopeful of mak- ing his battalion the strongest and most effi- cient in drill of any of the county battalions, and he expected the men to support him in that respect._ Following a tour round the town the men marched back to Bank Park. and Captain Rylands then expressed himself as being well pleased with the turn-out that morning. He added that, owing to the many particulars re- quired on the enrolment . forms, it would take too long for the men to be enrolled that morn- ing,' but parades would' be held every night that week for men to enter the particulars on their forms. The whole body would be sworn in after Church Parade next Sunday (to-Morrow) morning. He intended asking the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) to attend the swear- ing-in ceremony.

It is interesting to note that the Liverpool Volunteers were dressed in khaki, the men also being armed with service rifles and bayonets.

HORACE lIODGES' UNCLE.

Author-Actor Gets Points From A ticiative.

Last week "Foyer" had an interview with Mr. Horace Hodges, who was playing in "Grumpy" at the Royal Court Theatre, but, unfortunately, pressure of space squeezed it out of last Saturday's "Examiner." This is what he wrote:—

"Patrons this week have been thankful at having for their delectation an untruncated ver- sion of Grumpy.' For this they should thank Grumpy as heartily as Ernest, Heron does for the recovery of the diamond. 'For Mr. ,Horace Hodges is largely responsible. "Being part-author of the play he has some personal interest in its welfare, and he has seen several fine plays spoiled by being emasculated in order to be given twice nightly. " Grumpy " shall not be upset in that way if I can help it,' he said to me on Tuesday, which, by the way, was his 53rd birthday. Everyone was con- gratulating him. " Grumpy ' is a highly-successful play. He got the idea of ' Grumpy ' through observing an uncle who was an old doctor in Sussex, and with whom he used to spend many interesting holidays. Since his relative's death Mr. Hodges has been the owner of the house, and, whenever engagements permit, visits it. Always the idea of 'Grumpy' attends him. If he is not on the stage acting the old gentleman, he is resting amid the scenes where Grumpy lived and left such entertaining memories. . "As to playing 'Grumpy' in Warrington, Mr. Hodges remarked, 'We have had charming audiences. They have never missed a point. And what a nice theatre it is! It makes such a difference. Everyone this week has, in fact, been noticing with pleasure the wonderful im- provement in the theatre, its redecoration hav- ing been exquisitely done

"Being that it was his birthday, Mr. Hodges was especially quick at recollecting the past. He is an excellent conversationalist and

raconteur, and laughed heartily over the idea that the first time he made his appearance on the stage 'The Era' said, 'Mr. Hodges wa:; very bad.' Things have moved since then. Mr. Hodges toured with Wilson Barrett for 20 years and half as long with Fred Terry and Julia Neilson. Mr. Hodges has long been recognised as a brilliant actor. "Mr. Hodges has written several successful Plays. 'The first one Mr. Percy-cal (my col- laborator) and I ever did germinated in a Lon- don boarding-holl He hadn't any paper, so we asked the landlady for some. All that she could find Was the washing-book, and on the empty pages of that we wrote the melodrama. It didn't have a long run on the stage."

WORK TO SUIT VARICOSE VEINS.

At the Munitions Court on llondny, James Bell, employed by a local coal and iron Arm, sought for a certificate villich had been refused. Neither of the parties appeared, in court. In his statement, Bell set out that he suffered 'from varicose reins, and wished to leave in order to get a more suitable job. The firm wrote Stating that they are a controlled establishment and could not spare applicant. They were willing, however, to make arrangements to prevent him standing all the time he is ,at work. Under these circumstances the certificate was refused.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIZES PRESENTED AND COMPLI- MENTS PASSED.

At the conclusion of the games the prizes were plesented by Miss Margaret Broadbent

The Chairman of the-Association, Mr. F. Dean, said they had had a very successful main financially as well as from the playing point of view, the bowl- ing having been quite up to the usual good stan- dard. Subscriptions had been received from as low as sixpence to three guineas. The bowlers had done their part, for in the short space of a fort- night 431 entries were received, which was a record. (Applause.) The Secretary, Mr. J. Wood, then read letters of apology for non-attendance from Sir Arthur Cros- field, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. W. P. Rylands and Mr. C. E. Parker. Continuing, he said the com- petition had been a huge success, and in his work as secretary he had been nobly supported by all the, officials. He hardly knew how he would have got through the business bat for their help. Miss Broadbent then presented the prizes to the successful competitors, shaking hands with and complimenting each recipient.

On the pro—position of -Mr J. R. Ifoorfield, sec- onded by Mr. J. H. J. Hampson, a vote of thanks was accorded Miss Broadbent, the latter suitably replying. - -

Councillor G. Archer proposed a vote of thanks to the donors of the prizes, and Mr. W. Haigh seconded.

During the afternoon the Chairman of the Association presented cheques for £5 to four local hospitals, of which wounded soldiers are inmates. Mr. J. H. J. Hampson accepted the gift on behalf of the infirmary, Mr. W. Breward for the Thelwall Heys Auxiliary Hospital, Mr. R. Sewell for the Whitecross Hospital, and Miss Broadbent for Rad- don Court, all saying how very acceptable the gifts would be at the present time. The Secretary announced that the Association had decided to give £lO towards the Blind Sol- diers' Fund.

On the proposition of Mr. R. Sewell, a vote of thanks was accorded Mr. and Mrs. Starkey for the use of the green, and to the officials of the Associa- tion.

Mr. Starkey suitably reaponded, remarking that he was willing to do anything for charity.

CRICKET.

WARRINGTON AN XI V. ST. HELENS RECS. For the previous snatch a few weeks ago the local team were weakly represented for various reasons, having the greatest difficulty in raising a side to play the Recs., but to their credit fulfilled the fixture, although they received a severe drub- bing. In the return match last Saturday the visitors were practically at full strength, with the one exception, W. Howard, the fast bowler, being unable to make the journey owing to suffering a sad bereavement. T. Knight filled the vacancy. Warrington were fortunate in winning the toss, opening with G. Duckworth (20) and T. Ford. The *former improved on his previous good form, bat- ting with less restraint than usual, while his dis- play behind the wickets was much appreciated. T. Ford was snaking his first appearance for the visitors, and although not showing his true form, made a good impression. He comes from the Grappenhall C.C. with good credentials, and should prove a useful acquisition to the club, in view of the stiff engagements which have yet to be played. R. Stephens (15) also made a promising debut, pos- sessing a. very good style, and being an interesting player to watch.

The inclusion of J. Maiden (Dudley C.C.) was not a success,, although he is evidently a good bowler, but the conditions were against him, so he was relieved by T. E. Allen, who maintained a good length with little success. Compared with his all-round performance on the Viaduct enclosure, Allen had a bad day, having the misfortune to be badly run out.

In the last match on this ground F. Lemon (24) was dismissed without scoring. Last Saturday he met with a different mishap, receiving a painful injury when well set. He pluckily resumed, but had evidently not recovered from the effects of the accident, for he Tailed toy survive the next ball he received. After his dismissal a rot set in, the visitors losing five wickets cheaply. W. H. Bell (22 not out) then came to the rescue, and, along with T. Knight (26 not out), formed a useful partner- ship. adding fifty-six runs without being separated, despite the frequent changes of the St. Helens attack, and Constable then declared the innings. closed.

Owing to the late start and a stoppage for rain. which suspended play for well over an hour, the Recs. asked for half an hour extension, which was promptly allowed, thus giving the home team'one hundred minutes and a sporting chance. They opened carefully, J. Addison (21) and J. Tabern (74 not out) making sixty-five for the firpt wicket. W. Shaw (13) then joined J. Tabern, and after giving an easy chance off his first ball, which was not accepted. Bell clean bowled him with the score at 92.

Time was now fast approaching, when the cap- tain, J. E. Walker, came in, and they made strenuous efforts to win, hitting out recklessly. Lemon had the unenviable task of delivering the last over, and with only 16 runs wanted, any- thing was possible, the spectators getting very ex- cited, while the runs were registered as they were scored. They only added seven runs to their total, however, when the last ball of this exciting game bowled J. E. Walker (22), who had made a praise- worthy effort to win.

W. Bennett bowled throughout their innings. and, altrough having no luck, kept a beautiful length, and was always difficult to play. Scores:— Warrington Xl.—G. Duckworth c Starkey b Southward 20, T. Ford b Tabern 4, J. Maiden b Tabern 2, F. Lemon c J. Addison b Brooks 24, J. Constable b Brooks 8, G. Mann b Brooks 2, T. E. Allen run out 2, T. Knight not out 26, R. Stephens b Shaw 15, W. H. Bell not out 22, extras 15; total for eight wickets (innings declared) 140. St. Helens Recs.—J. Addison c Allen b Bennett 21, J. Tabern not out 74, W. Shaw b Bell 13, J. E. Walker. b Lemon 22, extras 1; total (for three wickets) 131. W. Southward, A. Addison, W. Starkey. H. Addison, B. Brooks, W. Baines and F. Eaves did not bat.

A Farmer's Joke:

Hitherto she had been a pretty little water- colour painting, crochet-working, piano-pound- ing sort of girl; now she was a farm worker.

At least se had been, earlier in The. day; at the moment there were signs of a strike, and she was giving the farmer a piece of her mind. "No," she said, in firm conclusion, "I don't mind shearing the pigs; or pruning the water- cress, or anything like that. But at mushroom- ing I draw the line. Why, my good man," she finished, with a maidenly blush, "I have never climbed a tree in my life !"
