THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 19, 1916.

Education.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY TRIBUNALS.

TIME-EXPIRED MEN.

Men Who Feel Sore At Being Called Up Again.

The human note was uppermost in the appeals of two ex-soldiers at the Tribunal at the Town Hall on Wednesday. A man of 22 years of age, who had been dis- charged from the Army as a time-expired sol- dier, after five years' service, including twelve months' in the trenches, was appealed for by Messrs. Edwin Richardson and Son, Ltd., who employ him as a grocer's packer. "These time-expired men feel very sore about being called up for service," said Mr. John Richardson. "-I have written to Colonel Churchill about it, but he had not the courtesy to reply. They have feud his speeches and are in a position to confirm his statement that there are hundreds of thousands of Men in France who have never been in the danger zone. They also feel annoyed that a million and a half men should be on the East Coast, when. Mr. Balfour tells us that invasion is a thing of the past since the victory off Jutland. These men have done their bit.",.

The Chairman (Councillor Joseph) : He is young enough to do another bit. Mr. Richardson: He is quite prepared to what he can in Red Cross or Special Constable work. Don't you think that these men ought to have some consideration on your part, if it is only a postponement? Captain Mathams : The claimant is asking you to set aside an Act of Parliament. However much individually they sympathised with time- expired men, they had to bear in mind that the country had spent several hundreds of pounds in training them and making them efficient soldiers. That being so, they were the very men who could help the country at the present moment; in fact, much better than any men they could possibly obtain. Although it might be hard, they were the men who were wanted and the most useful:

Mr. Richardson: Has the Tribunal no power to send these men to any particular service— home garrison duty, for instance? The Chairman: We have no such privilege. We have every sympathy with the young man, but we are afraid we have no alternative but to refuse the appeal. The best plan would be to take the case to the Court of Appeal and get a decision on the point, and we shan't be sorry if you are successful.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
Seventeen Years In The Army.

A single man, aged 36, who had 17 years' service in the Army, stated that when he was discharged in May he offered to re-enlist for a further four years, in order to qualify for a life pension, but the offer was not entertained. Alderman Tinnion : I cannot understand why the Army should release a man on May 27th and call him up again under the Military Service Act in August, especially when he offered himself for re-enlistment for a further term when discharged.' Captain Mathams pointed out that the re- enlistment could not be completed whilst the applicant was on service in France. He would get a bounty of .t2O on rejoining. Applicant: What is £2O in comparison to a life pension and seventeen years of service thrown away? My service is broken, and I cannot make it up again. Captain Mathams : If it can be preyed that he applied for re-enlistment before discharge that is a matter we can take up with the authorities.

The appeal was adjourned ' for further in- quiry.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE DISTRICT.

Appeals Heard From Farmers

And Business Men.

At Wednesday's sitting of the Warrington Rural District Tribunal' a Burtonwood farmer appealed for an employ, '3B years of age, mar- ried, who is highly-skilled in the working of agricultural machinery. The farm has 260 acres of arable land; WI he had four other men, one of whom is infirm. . Since putting in the appeal one of these had joined the Army. Applicant's three sons were with the colours.—Conditional exginption granted. An Orford tannery firnli;applied for exemption for a machinist, a married man, aged 32 years. It was explained that the man is a skilled machine-hand and is employed in finishing leather. If he had been in the firm's employ- ment when they were issuing badges they would have given him one. They had no single man whom they could let g4:F in hi i place.—The ap- peal was dismissed. The same firm sought exemption for two carters, one aged $4 and the other 33, both married. There were seven carters in all, the same number as the firm had six months ago. It was mentioned incidentally that 78 women are employed.—The Tribunal granted condi- tional exemption in the case of one of the men and the other appeal was dismissed. A fruit grower, aged 34, single, asked for a renewal of exemption on business and domestic grounds. He submitted that it would be a great hardship for his mother, who has chronic rheumatism, to be left alone on the premises at night. He' also claimed that his occupation was a certified one and that his orchard of fruit trees covered 71 acres. It was stated that temporary exemption had been granted to en- able applicant to make arrangements with his brother, who manages the Croft Brewery.— Further exeinption was allowed to the .end of September.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIBUNAL CRITICISED.

Rural Court's Adjournments

Out Of Order.

The Warrington Rural Tribunal came in for serious criticism at the Liverpool County Tribunal, on Tuesday, at the Town Hall, under the chairmanship of Alderman Peter Peacock (Mayor of .Warrington). Speaking on a complaint that the Warring- ton Rural Tribunal 'adjourned cases without coming to a definite decision, Captain Dickinson mentioned that in the past two months no fewer than 82 cases were adjourned by the Warrington .Rural Tribunal . without any defi- nite decision being arrived at. This wholesale adjourning was becoming a serious thing. He did not mind whether it was temporary or not, but they wanted a. definite decision in three cases.

The Chairman said the Tribunal were un- animous that in their opinion the Warrington Rural Tribunal were absolutely out of order. To adjourn these cases after having heard all the facts is out of order. They must decide one way or the other.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
A Friendly Society Secretary.

The District Secretary of a Warrington Friendly Society, for whom Mr. T. S. Steel appeared, appealed against the refusal of the Warrington Tribunal to grant him exemption. It was stated that he Was the acting-executor under the will of his father and that he had the sole management and collection of the pro- Pertf belonging to the estate and had to render an account annually to the beneficiaries.. He also had the management of .other property , belonging to a gentleman in the South of I England. He was the district secretary of the ' Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds' Friendly Society, with a membership of over 900. There were nine lodges in the Warrington district and he had to deal with the whole, of the cor- respondence of the district, and was responsible to the District Committee for the carrying out of the duties of the various lodge secretaries. He was also secretary 'of one of the lodges of the district with a membership of 180. He was preparing for the quinquennial valuations. and returns for the district, which required ,a very long examination of and extracts from the books of the district for the past five years. Apart from the voluntary side of the work of the society, he had charge of the State section. He was secretary of the Pastmasters' Lodge and also treasurer of the ,Friendly Societies' Medical Assapiation ' Warrington. Mr. Steel contended that the work on. which the appellant was engaged was work of national- I rin4pOrtance. - ,

, Affer considerable discussion.' the Mayor stated that the Tribunal had decided to allow the appeal by granting the appellant fourteen days' temporary exemption, which would really mean a month.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CHARLES DUKES.

Off To Chester With IVliiitary

Escort.

FLUTTER IN STOCKTON HEATH. ,

Mr. Charles Dukes, secretary of the Warring- ton Trade's and Labour Council and a member of the Stockton Heath Parish Council and Parochial Committee, whose appeals for exemp- tion. from military service On business and con- scientious grounds were dismissed ,by the Tri- bunals, was served with a 24 hours' notice on Thursday to•present himself at Chester Castle. He failed to do so and was arrested yesterday morning at his residence, 26, , Dundonald- avenue, Stockton Heath, by Inspector Breese, of the Cheshire Constabulary, on a charge of failing to appear at the time and place he was required to attend by the _military authorities.

_ A special Court was held at half-past two, when Mr. T. Baxter and M. W. Jackson Holmes presided, and defendant was charged with the offence named. As he came into Court in his reddish-brown Trades Council suit he recognised two or three friends and smiled reciprocally.

Inspector Breese said: At nine this morning I visited the prisoner's residence. I said "Why haven't you joined the Army?" He said "I don't intend to." I brought him to the Police Station and telephoned to the military authori- ties. He was asked again had he anything to say why he did not join and lie said "No." Inspector Breese then asked the Court to fine the defendant and make an order to hand him over to the military authorities. The escort, consisting of a sergeant and man equipped with bayonets, stood by ready. In reply to the Chairman (Mr. Baxter) the defendant admitted that he get the, notice to appear at Chester. He had nothing lo say,' except that he had no intcntion of joining the Army. The Chairman: You will be fined £2 for not attending to your duty. It will be deducted from your military pay if you get it; and you will be handed over to the military escort. Mr. Dukes, remarking "Is this the end of the proceedings?" then left the dock. During the few minutes occupied in hearing the case, there were tt number of people in Court and quite a crowd collected in the Square, outside the Police Station. It was found possible to catch the 3.15 train to Chester and Mr. George Seabury engaged a taxi, driven by a fair chauffeuse. Without any delay Mr. Dukes and his escort stepped briskly into the vehicle. One toot and they were off. The crowd, mostly composed of women, with chil- dren in their arms, jeered as the motor drove away.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SEND-OFF FROM BANK QUAY.

Mr. Dukes, his escort, and two trade union officials spent, the time of waiting for the train in the refreshment room. Here it was that an "Examiner" representative asked him if he had anything to say and he replied : "I should like to anpeal to working men to get ready for after-the-war problems which will confront , them. Stick to Trade Unionism. With strong unions the battles will be won much more easily. Employers are preparing for that day, and our side, too, must prepare." In the course of conversation he mentioned that it vvas rather curious that he should have lunch on the terrace of the House of Commons on Wednesday and have dinner in the cells at Stockton Heath on Friday. He was having breakfast when Inspector Breese called and that gentleman very courteously waited until he had finished. Farewell handshakes with his friends, who wished him "Good luck and cheer," and he and the men in khaki entered a carriage, in which were a clergyman, soldiers, and civilians —Church, Army and State. Private Dukes looked out of the window as the train moved out, waved a hand, and looked not in the least chagrined or unhappy.

The train was -b-O-und for Llandudno and other holiday resorts; but it stopped at Chester.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
Tribunal Appeal Raised In The

Commons.

NO ALTERATION.

In the House of Commons, on Monday, Mr. Richard Lambert asked the President of the Local Government Board whether he was aware that the Salford Hundred Appeal Tribunal on The Bth inst. refused the appeal of Mr. Charles Dukes, East Lancashire organiser of the Gas- workers' and General Labourers' Union, al- though his anti-militarist views have been well known in the district for years, and what action he proposes to take. The Minister, in reply, said he was 'aware of the procedure of the Tribunal. The appeal was made to the Tribunal on conscientious grounds, and the Tribunal were obviously in a better position than himself to go into the particulars of the case.

ARREST OF WARRINGTON CLERKS.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday,

Mr. Snowden asked the Secretary of State for War if Ile would say what action he has taken or proposes to take upon the conduct of the re- cruiting officers at Warrington who have caused the arrest of two clerks named Wright and Sanders, employed by the Cheshire Lines Com- mittee at Warrington, as absentees under the Military Service Act, seeing that their cases are before the Labour Advisory Committee on Industrial Conscription, it being alleged that these two clerks are the victims of, persecution and discrimination by the railway company. Mr. Forster: The cases of these two men have been brought to the .notice of the War Office, and are the subject of inquiry. I do not think any blame attaches to the recruiting officers for causing them to be dealt with as absentees, but, as I have said, the cases are being inquired into.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SECOND-FOURTH.

Coming Home For Holiday On Tuesday.

After being away for over two years ;the majority of those in the Second-Fourth South Lancashire Regiment are coming to Warring- ton for four days' leave. They are due to arrive at Bank Quay Railway Station about six o'clock on Tuesday evening, when a rousing reception may be anticipated. That they will enjoy their stay in the town is the wish of all, and it is gratifying to note that, with a view to this being so, Colonel Egerton. Fairclough, Colonel Dutton Fairclough and, the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) are interesting themselves in this direction. They will be received by the Band of the Third-Fourth South Lancashire Regiment. On Wednesday afternoon Mr. Jackson will submit to the men a "top-hole" programme in the Palace and Hippodrome, and in 'the evening there will be a dance in the Parr Hall.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
MISSING.

PRIVATES SAM BENNION AND EDWARD BOWERS.

His brother Joseph has sent word to Mr. and Mrs. Bennion, of 268, Lovely-lane, that Private Samuel Bennion has been missing for several (lays. He is in the King's (Liverpool)` Regi- ment. Both brothers have been serving to- gether almost since the commencement of the war.

Great anxiety is also felt regarding the fate of Private Edward Bowers, of the same regi- ment, and who resided. with his sister, Mrs. Arthur Sinclair, in Longdin-street, Latchford. A friend has written stating that he saw him go into a charge, but has not heard of him since. Private Bowers formerly worked for Messrs. Coates, .Bridge-street, and was well- known at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

STAGE AND KINEMA.

Royal Court Theatre.

The succes4,, experienced elsewhere has also been evident in Warrington this week in the production of "Mr. Wu" by Mr. J. Bannister Howard's principal company. Of splendid quality and possessing in addition the fascina- tion of Oriental ideas and atmosphere, the play has for its principal actor Mr. Edgar G. Wood, a young actor of great ability. Miss Helen Saintsbury, who acts Nan Ping, his daughter, is his wife. They first met in "Mr. Wu," and were married about last Christmas.

A Capital " twice-nighter " is promised for next week, when Messrs. Horsfield and Wood- ward will present "Within the Law," a fine play, full of vigour and incident. The plot is keenly written and the scenes are cleverly laid, maintaining the interest of the audience throughout. Miss Lilian Hallows should be seen in the part of Margaret Taylor, one which affords excellent opportunities for displaying her exceptional talents. The whole cast is one of considerable merit.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
Palace and Hippodrome.

"The Mystery Gun," presented by Mr. Fred Clayden, is an interesting illusion., Miss Erica O'Foyle: (the captain's daughter) is apparently shot from an imposing piece of artillery, and re-appears unhurt and smiling and bowing to the audience.

An amusing comedy is written round this ex- citing incident, and there is a good deal of fun on the battleship's gun deck. "Lively A. 8." (Fred Clayden, jun.) is as good as his name, and when a shore signal announces the arrival of triplets he rapidly calculates, "Three little Lloyd Georges and the King's bounty—seven pound ten.'s.

"The Golden Athlones" appear in a striking series of tableaux. The trio of artistes are covered from head to foot with a solution which give the appearance of golden statues, and with the assistance of cleverly worked lights the various groupings are dazzlingly effective. The trio included Miss Doris Valento—the girl with the perfect.figure.

A feast of good things is promised for next week. Heading the bill are the Zylbastrians, clever instrumentalists; while the Six Ceylons will appear in "Fun in an Opium Den." Other chief attractions are Gus Garrick, humorist; and Maggie Clifton and Partner, dazzling gymnasts.

===== 0002601_19160819_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
Kinemas.

At the Star Kinema there is now showing a fine modern drama, entitled "Samson," which grips one from start to finish. All next week Were will he thrown on the screen exclusive scenes taken at the Warrington Navy Day by the "Star" photographer. During the first half of the week there will also be shown a fine picture, entitled "The Two Orphans," while a powerful drama, "The Plunderer," will occupy premier position during the latter half.

At the Pavilion, Sankey Green, the third ad- venture of Terence O'Rourke now holds sway, while for the first half of next week a fine exclusive picture; "The White Scar," will be screened, as also will the twelfth episode of "The Diamond from the Sky." During the latter half "The Princes of the Tower" will be shown, along with the fourth episode of "Terence O'Rourke."

At the Grand Electric Theatre "Golden Lotus" is now attracting crowded houses, while next week's "star" pictures are "Der Heart's Desire," for the first half, and the twelfth episode of "The Diamond from the Sky" and "Uncle Tom's Cabin" for the latter half.

"Greed" is now being screened at the Cen- tral, at which house the tenth episode of "The Diamond from the Sky" will be shown (luring the first half of next week, and " Greed" dur- ing the latter half.

MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

To-morrow's Concerts In Park And Hippodrome.

The eighth annual festival in aid of the Warrington Infirmary (and on this occasion of Navy Day) held under the auspices of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee, takes place to-morrow (Sunday) in the Park and the Hippodrome.

The open-air festival will . begin at three o'clock, the Mayor having arranged for a flag to float from the Town Hall if the weather has been of such a character as to allow of the gathering taking place in the Park. If no flag flies it will be an indication that the festival has been transferred to Parr Hall. By kind Permission of Colonel J. L. Vaughan, the Band of the 2nd 'Battalion South Lancashire Regi- ment, under the conductorship of Mr. R. E. Cahill, will be in attendance, and Messrs. J. H. Draper and Arthur C. Pearce will be the vocalists, Mr. W. H. Payton being the accom- panist. The following is the very fine pro- gramme arranged :

Hymn, "0 -God, our help in ages past"; overture, "Saul" (Bazzini); song, "The Trumpeter" (Dix), Mr. J. H. Draper; selec- tion, "Cavalleria Rusticana" (Mascagni); song, "Is not His word like a fire?" ("Elijah"), Mr. Arthur C. Pearce; selection of the works of Sullivan (Winterbottom); duet, "Watchman! What of the night?" (Serjeant), Messrs. J. H. Draper and Arthur C. Pearce; Norwegian rhapsody (Lab()); hymn, "Abide with me"; "God save the King."

Mr. Payton will again accompany.

TIIE NEEDS OF THE INFIRMARY.

At a meeting of the Working Men's Com- mittee, at winch the above arrangements were confirmed (Mr. Daintith . in the chair), Mr. Hampson, the hon, secretary, reported that there were now 52 wounded soldiers in the In- firmary, and that otherwise the institution was well occupied. There was, he said, every reason why the public should support the institution more than ever, the increased price of provi- sions having added materially to the cost of maintenance. •

A vote of condolence was passed with Alder- man T. Burton at the loss of his eldest son, Mr. Wm. Burton. Representatives of the Post Office on the com- mittee were welcomed by Mr. George Walker (who arrived during the progress of the meet- ing) and Mr. Daintith. The Annual Concert.

RAILWAY FARE ALLOWED.

STORIES OF THE WAR.

More Religion In The Army Than Out

01 It.

Mr. W. J. Leaper, a member of the editorial staff of "The Examiner," writing from the Front, says

" W-arri-ngton.. and district, I see, has lost heavily in the offensive, but if victory is worth having, it must be paid for. It is not mY privilege to be able to take a part in the fighting—my eyes prevent, that—but our Batta- lion is doing very useful work constructing a railway as our armies advance.

"Our men out here are splendid and our artillery is more than a match for the Ger- mans. The other evening, or rather night, I could not sleep owing to neuralgia, so I went out of the tent to watch the bombardment Of the German lines. Whilst out I witnessed 11 scene I shall never forget. The guns were rumbling as fast as machine guns and the skY was lit up by the flashes of the heavy artillery- As I stood under the trees .an ammunition kolumn passed me. The drivers were singing, not 'Keep the Home Fires Burning' or the latest revue song, but the hymn, 'Thy Will Be Done.' I marvelled at their spirit and felt that there is more real religion in the Army -than out of it. I tell you these things so that your confidence in victory will be strengthened, if it needs strengthening. I myself think the end is not far off, for it is impossible 'for men to live under the rain of shells our artillery send, over every night. So, hoping to see you al' again before long.. . ."

David Anderson, who is serving with the Rifle Brigade in France, to the ettect that he has been gassed and has lost his speech in the recent severe fighting,, and is now in a general. hospi- tal. This is the fourth occasion 'on which Rifle- man. Anderson has been admitted to hospital in France. The first time was after the battle of Loos, when he was wounded in the leg; later with trench disease, and on June 6th of the present year he was blown up by a mine explo- sion. On this occasion, after being extricated, he succeeded, along with others,. in saving his platoon officer, for which .act he has been recom- mended for some distinction. Rifleman Anderson is a native of Edin)nirrh. Prior to the outbreak' of war he was employe.l as a boilerman at Messrs. Crosfield's, Ltd. He joined the Army in September, 1914.

CYCLING AND MOTORING. SOME MUDGUARD IMPROVEMENTS. A good many cyclists are in the habit of remov- ing their mudguards during the summer menthe. The only reason for doing this, so far as I know, is to lighten the machine. But surely this is dearly bought lightness, and, personally, I should never think of removing these most necessary accessories. How many times have we not all seen, after a heavy shower of rain, a cyclist plugging along through the mud on a machine minus mudguards, who was covered with mud from head to foot, back and front. Our climate, you know, Aon't stand any liberties being taken with it, and it is merely ask- ing for trouble to go out even on the finest of sum- mer days without adequate mudguards. To .make mudguards really effective there are two additions I would suggest, both to the front one. An exten-* sion, some nine inches in length, extending from the fork crown over the top of the front wheel, prevents the mud being blown on to the rider. It is not an unsightly attachment, but, adds rather to the appearance of the machine. To hinder the boots becoming muddy, a pear-shaped piece of leather, about seven or eight inches wide, fastened to the bottom of the front mudguard, and coming to within three inches of the ground, is most ser- viceable. WHEN THE MAGNETO GETS WET. It is by no means an uncommon occurrence when motor cycling in heavy rain for the water to run down the high tension wire on to the magneto, causing a short circuit and an unwelcome stop- page. The only thing to do is to trace the "short" to its lair and get rid of the trouble as soon as pos- sible. But whatever you do, don't attempt to do it out in the rain, for if you do more water will in- evitably enter and the last state will be infinitely worse than the first. You must get under shelter somewhere; a bridge, barn, tree, or, at the worst, a sheltered wall can usually be found within a few hundred yards; if not, spread your mackintosh over the saddle and work under its protection. Dry everi, part thoroughly as you remove it, from the high tension terminal onwards, and if you have

such a thing by you, smear all the metal pate with vaseline before you replace them. Don't for- get to go over the switch wire, if one happens to be fitted. It is a most fruitful source of trouble, as it is in contact with metal over practically the whole of its journey and is none too well insulated. Remove altogether in case of doubt and drive on the exhaust lifter. All wires have a habit of play- ing funny tricks in wet weather, while behaving quite rationally on a fine day. It is very iii- probable that the short circuit is right inside the magneto; '99 times' in 100 the trouble is merely a surface one, and a dab over everything with a di"' cloth will put things to rights. It must be remem- bered that water is an almost perfect conductor of electricity. HILL CLIMINNG. When the gradient is severe, and the load heavy one, a good deal of judgment is necessary oil the part of motor cyclists to negotiate the hill successfully. There is a right way and a wrong way of doing it. Always commence the hill with ao great a speed as is consistent with safety, taking everything into consideration, and have the throttle well opened, giving as much air as possible. AS soon ai the machine begins to slow down the second gear should be employed, and, as a general rule, the throttle should be slightly closed, but earl be opened again shortly afterwards. If a Per- ceptible slowing down is then noticed, drop into low gear, slightly closing, then open the throttle as required. The object of closing the throttle directly after changing gear is to prevent the engine racing. Don't wait until the motor cycle ie running slow before changing, but do this when it begins to slow. Never stint the supply of oil when climbing a hill. SPOKES.

Otburrh actus. REV. J. MARTIN'S FAREWELL. The Rev. J. Martin completed his ministry at St. Peter's Church on Sunday evening, when he preached his farewell sermon to a large congregation. The reverend gentleman, who has accepted the curacy of Emmanuel Church, Pazakerley, has been a diligent worker, and was greatly esteemed by all the parishioners. NATIONAL MISSION. Admiral Beatty On England's Self- Satisfaction. Surely the Almighty God does not intend this war to be a hideous fracas—a blood drunken orgy. There must be a purpose in it all. Im- provement must be born out of it: in w•iat direction? France has already shown us the way, and has risen out of her ruined cities with a revival of religion that is most wonderful. Russia has been welded into a whole, and religion plays the greater part. England still remains to be dug out of the stupor of self-satisfaction and complacency, which her great and flourishing condition has steeped her in. And until she can be stirred out of this condition, until a religious revival takes place at home, just so long will the war continue. When she can look out on the future with humbler eyes and a prayer on hee lips, then we can begin to count the days towards the end.— DAVID BEATTY.

W. Herbert, of: the South Lancashires, had been killed, in action. "I feel I must write to tell you the very, sad news that George was killed in action. No words of mine can express the sorrow we all feel at his loss. I' had. known him all through my soldiering experience, and have always found ,him one ,of the best.' He has been called Upon to make the supreme sacrifice, and wh9 shall say that in the here- after he shall go unrewarded? I trust it may be some consolation to you to know that George died for his King and country." Corporal Herbert was the son of Mr. ,and Mrs. G. Herbert, and before joining up he worked for the Pearson and Knowles Coal- and Iron Co., Ltd., at Dallam Forge. He was 29 years of age, attended St. Paul's Church, and was an old boy of Wycliffe School. DRIVER C. PHIBBS. Delivered His Ammunition Before Being Struck. • Driver. C. GUNNER FINCH. Died.

and mother, Mr. and Mrs. Finch, at 57, Cart- wright-street, has died of disease in the East. Official intimation of his death has been re- ceived from the War Office. Gunner Finch was educated at Wycliffe School and attended St. Barnabas' Sunday school. He was only 19 years of age, and had been in the East 12 months.

RUSSIA'S CAPTURES. The Russians published this week the gigantic total of their captures from the beginning of their offensive in June to August 12. It amounts to 7,757 officers, 350,845 men, 405 cannon; 1,326 machine guns, with hundreds of bomb-throvrers and caissons. A FURTHER LIST. A supplementary total given on Friday shcsys that about 7,500 officers and men have fallel,l, into the hands of the Russians in the reoen; Operations. These were captured by General Bezobrazoff, a commander who has not been named before, but who is presumably acting under General Shcherbacheff between the Dnies- ter and the Sereth. German Sausage. The German Socialist " Vorwaerts" reports that a dealer in fat goods at Hamburg, named Keller, exposed for sale a "very fine liver sausage" at 2.20 marks per pound. The sausage did not contain the smallest trace of liver, meat, or fat. It consisted of finely-cut Pieee_i9 of indiarubber, and of a gluey slime, with piec of hair cut small or ground. IN THE COURTS. FINES • FOR LIGHTS INSTEAD OF DARKNESS. At the Borough Police Court on Monday John Greenwood, butcher, 131, Sankey-street, was summoned by Acting-Sergeant Whitby for fail- ing to reduce the light in his premises on the 3rd August after 11 p.m. Defendant said the decorators had been in the shop and failed to pull down the blind.—Fined ss. ' The case against Horace Brown was of a more serious nature, as, according to the evidence of Acting-Sergeant Armstrong, two bright electric lights were left burning all night in his shop, 24, Horsemarket-street. The light was partially shining up Lyme-street. Defendant had no ex cuse to offer except that the man whom he had left in the shop forgot to turn off the lights.— A fine of 295. was imposed. A REFORMATORY CASE. Ex-Boy-Soldier Breaks Open A Slot • Meter. A boy was charged in the Borough Police Court on Monday with having broken open a gas slot meter, the property of the Corporation, in 22, Queen-street, and with stealing 17s. from Same. Chief Constable Nicholls said he would ask the Bench to send defendant to a Reformatory SchooL lie would attain the age of sixteen years the following day, and, therefore, the commitment should be made out at once. When the prisoner was seen first of all by Detective Turner he denied having broken the lock off the meter and said that he knocked it off acci- dentally with a hamper as he passed by, but he afterwards admitted forcing the lock off with a turnsgrew. Some time ago defendant was convicted of stealing biscuits, for which he was birched. His mother was absolutaly incapable of lookbig after the boy—she was a hard-work- ing, respectable woman and had no control over 'him. Early in the year he enlisted, but soon after wrote to his mother threatening what he would not do unless she claimed his discharge. She claimed his discharge and he left the Army some months ago. Since then he had done nothing at all. He asked the magistrates to commit him to a Reformatory. He would only have three years' training, but it appeared to be absolutely the only solution in the boy's own interest. Defendant, who had nothing to say, was then committed to a Reformatory until he should reach the age of nineteen years. SPOILING FARMER'S CROP. Eight Warrington boys having recently made a raid on a potato crop at Buttonwood, the property of Mr. Warburton, farmer, when they did damage to the, extent of 55., appeared at the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, charged with that offence. Superintendent Forster pointed out that the police and farmers in the Sankey and Burton- wood district had had much trouble with such cases, considerable damage haying been done. In fining, the defendants, the Chairman of the Bench (Mr. J. Ball) said suck conduct was becoming a regular nuisance and a disgrace. Two of the boys were fined 2s. 6d. each and the other six, who had been previously con- victed, 10s. each.. DIFFERENT FROM ANYONE ELSE WHEN DRIVING THE MAIL. Charged at the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednesday, with driving a motor. car at Penketh without head lights, and also with failing to produce, his licence, Patrick Maginn, of Warrington, was fined X/ in each case. He pleaded "Not guilty" to the first charge and guilty to the second. P.C. Hopson said that at 9.50 p.m. on Mrin- day, the 7th inst., he was on duty in Warring- ton-road, Penketh, when he saw the defendant in charge of a light motor car, which had no front lights attached. The car stopped near the Post Office, and, upon being asked why fie had no head-lights, the defendant replied: "I am different from anyone else when driving the mail." Asked to produce his licence he said: "I will not show you my licence; you must go to the Post Office for it." The defendant now said that he was lighting the lamp when the constable came up, but this statement the Constable denied, saying the defendant was seated in the car when he approached: The lamp was quite cold. Superintendent Foster said the defendant had been eleven times previously before the Courts.
